========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hello, Doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Well, I suppose I should start by saying that I'm a secondary school teacher, so I am very used to being on my feet all day. I teach history, and I've always loved the energy of the classroom, pacing around, keeping the teenagers engaged. But what actually brought me in today is... well, it's becoming quite embarrassing, to be honest. I just keep getting up when I'm supposed to be sitting down. 

It happens down at the school during our staff meetings, and even in my personal life, like at dinner parties when everyone else is perfectly content just remaining seated. I’ll be sitting there, and suddenly I just feel this need to move, so I'll invent a reason to get up. I'll pop up to refill my coffee—even if my cup is still half full, which is ridiculous—or I'll suddenly decide I need to go check a handout on the other side of the room. Or sometimes I just walk to the back of the room and stand there for a while! And the thing is, none of it is ever really urgent. 

My husband actually pointed it out at a dinner last week. We were at our friends' house, we had just finished the main course, and I just stood up to go look at a painting on their wall. I mean, who does that? Everyone else is sitting, having a lovely conversation, and I'm wandering around their dining room. It's just... I don't know, I'm 60 years old, people expect me to be able to sit through a normal, quiet event, but it's like my legs just decide it's time to go and I can't stay in my chair without finding some excuse to be on my feet.

Well, it’s certainly not a pain or a physical ache, if that’s what you mean. It’s more of a... a buzzing, I suppose? Or just this incredible restlessness that sort of builds up. If I try to ignore it and just force myself to stay in the chair—because, as I said, sometimes you really just can't get up, no matter how much you want to. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays. Oh, the essays. I'll be halfway through a massive stack of papers on the Industrial Revolution, trying to focus on what my students have written, and I'll suddenly realize I'm just constantly in motion. 

I’ll be tapping my foot against the back of the desk, just tap-tap-tapping away, or I'll be clicking my red grading pen without even realizing I'm doing it. Click, click, click. And I'm shifting my weight from one hip to the other, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, adjusting my posture... it's exhausting! 

It’s the exact same thing when I’m in church on a Sunday, which is terribly embarrassing. I’ll be sitting in the pew during the vicar's sermon, and I just squirm and shift around so much that I literally have to have a stern internal monologue and remind myself to just settle down. I’ll think, 'For goodness sake, you’re a grown woman, sit still!' But it’s just this restless energy that absolutely has to go somewhere, so if I can't stand up and walk it off like I want to, it just sort of leaks out into my feet and my hands and I end up fidgeting the entire time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not really... I mean, I wouldn't say it's an evening thing specifically, or even that it only happens after I've been sitting for ages. It's not like that at all. It's more just... whenever I'm expected to sit, really. It could be first thing in the morning! We have our morning staff briefings at 8:00 AM sharp, and sometimes I've barely sat down with my tea, and within five minutes, I'm already feeling that sort of buzzing start up in my legs. I'll be sitting there, listening to the headmaster drone on about the new attendance policy or whatever it is that day, and I'm already shifting around, thinking about getting up to look at the notice board, even though I just looked at it on my way in. 

And then, of course, it happens in the evening too, like at those dinner parties I mentioned, or if my husband and I are just trying to sit and watch a program on the television after dinner. We love those historical dramas, you know, being a history teacher and all, but even then, I can't just sink into the sofa and stay there for an hour. I'll be up checking the kettle, or deciding I need to fold the laundry right that second. 

So, it's not really tied to the time of day, and I don't have to be sitting for hours and hours for it to start. It's just the very act of having to stay seated when my body clearly wants to be doing something else. It's like my internal engine is just always idling a bit too high, whether it's breakfast time or supper time, whether I've been sitting for five minutes or fifty. It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!

If I try to ignore it and just force myself to stay in the chair—because, as I said, sometimes you really just can't get up, no matter how much you want to. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays. Oh, the essays. I'll be halfway through a massive stack of papers on the Industrial Revolution, trying to focus on what my students have written, and I'll suddenly realize I'm just constantly in motion. \n\nI’ll be tapping my foot against the back of the desk, just tap-tap-tapping away, or I'll be clicking my red grading pen without even realizing I'm doing it. Click, click, click. And I'm shifting my weight from one hip to the other, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, adjusting my posture... it's exhausting! \n\nIt’s the exact same thing when I’m in church on a Sunday, which is terribly embarrassing. I’ll be sitting in the pew during the vicar's sermon, and I just squirm and shift around so much that I literally have to have a stern internal monologue and remind myself to just settle down. I’ll think, 'For goodness sake, you’re a grown woman, sit still!' But it’s just this restless energy that absolutely has to go somewhere, so if I can't stand up and walk it off like I want to, it just sort of leaks out into my feet and my hands and I end up fidgeting the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling in your legs tend to happen more in the evening, or after you've been sitting for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really... I mean, I wouldn't say it's an evening thing specifically, or even that it only happens after I've been sitting for ages. It's not like that at all. It's more just... whenever I'm expected to sit, really. It could be first thing in the morning! We have our morning staff briefings at 8:00 AM sharp, and sometimes I've barely sat down with my tea, and within five minutes, I'm already feeling that sort of buzzing start up in my legs. I'll be sitting there, listening to the headmaster drone on about the new attendance policy or whatever it is that day, and I'm already shifting around, thinking about getting up to look at the notice board, even though I just looked at it on my way in. \n\nAnd then, of course, it happens in the evening too, like at those dinner parties I mentioned, or if my husband and I are just trying to sit and watch a program on the television after dinner. We love those historical dramas, you know, being a history teacher and all, but even then, I can't just sink into the sofa and stay there for an hour. I'll be up checking the kettle, or deciding I need to fold the laundry right that second. \n\nSo, it's not really tied to the time of day, and I don't have to be sitting for hours and hours for it to start. It's just the very act of having to stay seated when my body clearly wants to be doing something else. It's like my internal engine is just always idling a bit too high, whether it's breakfast time or supper time, whether I've been sitting for five minutes or fifty. It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I suppose it does, in a way... but not like a magical cure or anything. It’s more that when I finally do get up—like when I invent that excuse to go check the coffee pot or wander over to the notice board during the staff briefing—it’s as if a pressure valve has been released, just for a moment. But it’s not as if the restlessness completely disappears forever just because I took a little walk across the room. 

I mean, yes, while I'm actually walking, while I'm moving, I feel much more... well, normal, I suppose. I feel like myself. When I’m pacing at the front of my classroom, lecturing about the Tudor dynasty, I feel absolutely fine! I don't even think about my legs. But the second I have to sit back down, say, if the students are taking an exam and I need to supervise from my desk... oh, it just starts right back up again. The buzzing, the tapping, the need to shift. 

So, yes, the moving around definitely helps while I'm doing it. If I could just stay in motion all day, I probably wouldn't even be here talking to you! But the problem is, society expects a 60-year-old woman to sit down eventually, doesn't it? And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n4\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nG21.3, F42.2, F43.1, F43.2, F44.4, F44.5, F54\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Well, I suppose I should start by saying that I'm a secondary school teacher, so I am very used to being on my feet all day. I teach history, and I've always loved the energy of the classroom, pacing around, keeping the teenagers engaged. But what actually brought me in today is... well, it's becoming quite embarrassing, to be honest. I just keep getting up when I'm supposed to be sitting down. \n\nIt happens down at the school during our staff meetings, and even in my personal life, like at dinner parties when everyone else is perfectly content just remaining seated. I’ll be sitting there, and suddenly I just feel this need to move, so I'll invent a reason to get up. I'll pop up to refill my coffee—even if my cup is still half full, which is ridiculous—or I'll suddenly decide I need to go check a handout on the other side of the room. Or sometimes I just walk to the back of the room and stand there for a while! And the thing is, none of it is ever really urgent. \n\nMy husband actually pointed it out at a dinner last week. We were at our friends' house, we had just finished the main course, and I just stood up to go look at a painting on their wall. I mean, who does that? Everyone else is sitting, having a lovely conversation, and I'm wandering around their dining room. It's just... I don't know, I'm 60 years old, people expect me to be able to sit through a normal, quiet event, but it's like my legs just decide it's time to go and I can't stay in my chair without finding some excuse to be on my feet.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you’re feeling in your legs when you get the urge to stand up? Is it more of a restlessness, discomfort, or something else that makes it hard to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s certainly not a pain or a physical ache, if that’s what you mean. It’s more of a... a buzzing, I suppose? Or just this incredible restlessness that sort of builds up. If I try to ignore it and just force myself to stay in the chair—because, as I said, sometimes you really just can't get up, no matter how much you want to. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays. Oh, the essays. I'll be halfway through a massive stack of papers on the Industrial Revolution, trying to focus on what my students have written, and I'll suddenly realize I'm just constantly in motion. \n\nI’ll be tapping my foot against the back of the desk, just tap-tap-tapping away, or I'll be clicking my red grading pen without even realizing I'm doing it. Click, click, click. And I'm shifting my weight from one hip to the other, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, adjusting my posture... it's exhausting! \n\nIt’s the exact same thing when I’m in church on a Sunday, which is terribly embarrassing. I’ll be sitting in the pew during the vicar's sermon, and I just squirm and shift around so much that I literally have to have a stern internal monologue and remind myself to just settle down. I’ll think, 'For goodness sake, you’re a grown woman, sit still!' But it’s just this restless energy that absolutely has to go somewhere, so if I can't stand up and walk it off like I want to, it just sort of leaks out into my feet and my hands and I end up fidgeting the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling in your legs tend to happen more in the evening, or after you've been sitting for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really... I mean, I wouldn't say it's an evening thing specifically, or even that it only happens after I've been sitting for ages. It's not like that at all. It's more just... whenever I'm expected to sit, really. It could be first thing in the morning! We have our morning staff briefings at 8:00 AM sharp, and sometimes I've barely sat down with my tea, and within five minutes, I'm already feeling that sort of buzzing start up in my legs. I'll be sitting there, listening to the headmaster drone on about the new attendance policy or whatever it is that day, and I'm already shifting around, thinking about getting up to look at the notice board, even though I just looked at it on my way in. \n\nAnd then, of course, it happens in the evening too, like at those dinner parties I mentioned, or if my husband and I are just trying to sit and watch a program on the television after dinner. We love those historical dramas, you know, being a history teacher and all, but even then, I can't just sink into the sofa and stay there for an hour. I'll be up checking the kettle, or deciding I need to fold the laundry right that second. \n\nSo, it's not really tied to the time of day, and I don't have to be sitting for hours and hours for it to start. It's just the very act of having to stay seated when my body clearly wants to be doing something else. It's like my internal engine is just always idling a bit too high, whether it's breakfast time or supper time, whether I've been sitting for five minutes or fifty. It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!\n\nDoctor: Does the restless feeling in your legs get better when you stand up or move around?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does, in a way... but not like a magical cure or anything. It’s more that when I finally do get up—like when I invent that excuse to go check the coffee pot or wander over to the notice board during the staff briefing—it’s as if a pressure valve has been released, just for a moment. But it’s not as if the restlessness completely disappears forever just because I took a little walk across the room. \n\nI mean, yes, while I'm actually walking, while I'm moving, I feel much more... well, normal, I suppose. I feel like myself. When I’m pacing at the front of my classroom, lecturing about the Tudor dynasty, I feel absolutely fine! I don't even think about my legs. But the second I have to sit back down, say, if the students are taking an exam and I need to supervise from my desk... oh, it just starts right back up again. The buzzing, the tapping, the need to shift. \n\nSo, yes, the moving around definitely helps while I'm doing it. If I could just stay in motion all day, I probably wouldn't even be here talking to you! But the problem is, society expects a 60-year-old woman to sit down eventually, doesn't it? And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!\n\nDoctor: Does the restless feeling in your legs get better when you stand up or move around?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does, in a way... but not like a magical cure or anything. It’s more that when I finally do get up—like when I invent that excuse to go check the coffee pot or wander over to the notice board during the staff briefing—it’s as if a pressure valve has been released, just for a moment. But it’s not as if the restlessness completely disappears forever just because I took a little walk across the room. \n\nI mean, yes, while I'm actually walking, while I'm moving, I feel much more... well, normal, I suppose. I feel like myself. When I’m pacing at the front of my classroom, lecturing about the Tudor dynasty, I feel absolutely fine! I don't even think about my legs. But the second I have to sit back down, say, if the students are taking an exam and I need to supervise from my desk... oh, it just starts right back up again. The buzzing, the tapping, the need to shift. \n\nSo, yes, the moving around definitely helps while I'm doing it. If I could just stay in motion all day, I probably wouldn't even be here talking to you! But the problem is, society expects a 60-year-old woman to sit down eventually, doesn't it? And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you feel that restlessness in your legs, is there ever any discomfort or an uncomfortable sensation—like aching, tingling, or crawling—that makes you want to move them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, nothing like that at all. It’s not a... a physical discomfort, if you see what I mean. Not an ache, certainly not a tingling, and definitely not that awful crawling feeling you hear people talk about. I had a colleague once, a lovely woman who taught geography, who had something like that—she said it felt like spiders in her veins, which just sounds absolutely dreadful, doesn't it? But no, it’s nothing like that for me. 

It’s really just... energy. Pure, unadulterated energy that simply has no place to go. It’s not painful in the slightest, just incredibly insistent. It’s as if my legs are saying, "Right then, we're done sitting, let's get on with it!" even if my brain is saying, "Please, just let me finish marking this one last essay on the French Revolution." There’s no physical sensation of illness or ache, just this overwhelming, bubbling drive to be doing something else, somewhere else, preferably on my feet. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.

But what actually brought me in today is... well, it's becoming quite embarrassing, to be honest. I just keep getting up when I'm supposed to be sitting down. \n\nIt happens down at the school during our staff meetings, and even in my personal life, like at dinner parties when everyone else is perfectly content just remaining seated. I’ll be sitting there, and suddenly I just feel this need to move, so I'll invent a reason to get up. I'll pop up to refill my coffee—even if my cup is still half full, which is ridiculous—or I'll suddenly decide I need to go check a handout on the other side of the room. Or sometimes I just walk to the back of the room and stand there for a while! And the thing is, none of it is ever really urgent. \n\nMy husband actually pointed it out at a dinner last week. We were at our friends' house, we had just finished the main course, and I just stood up to go look at a painting on their wall. I mean, who does that? Everyone else is sitting, having a lovely conversation, and I'm wandering around their dining room. It's just... I don't know, I'm 60 years old, people expect me to be able to sit through a normal, quiet event, but it's like my legs just decide it's time to go and I can't stay in my chair without finding some excuse to be on my feet.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you’re feeling in your legs when you get the urge to stand up? Is it more of a restlessness, discomfort, or something else that makes it hard to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s certainly not a pain or a physical ache, if that’s what you mean. It’s more of a... a buzzing, I suppose? Or just this incredible restlessness that sort of builds up. If I try to ignore it and just force myself to stay in the chair—because, as I said, sometimes you really just can't get up, no matter how much you want to. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays. Oh, the essays. I'll be halfway through a massive stack of papers on the Industrial Revolution, trying to focus on what my students have written, and I'll suddenly realize I'm just constantly in motion. \n\nI’ll be tapping my foot against the back of the desk, just tap-tap-tapping away, or I'll be clicking my red grading pen without even realizing I'm doing it. Click, click, click. And I'm shifting my weight from one hip to the other, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, adjusting my posture... it's exhausting! \n\nIt’s the exact same thing when I’m in church on a Sunday, which is terribly embarrassing. I’ll be sitting in the pew during the vicar's sermon, and I just squirm and shift around so much that I literally have to have a stern internal monologue and remind myself to just settle down. I’ll think, 'For goodness sake, you’re a grown woman, sit still!' But it’s just this restless energy that absolutely has to go somewhere, so if I can't stand up and walk it off like I want to, it just sort of leaks out into my feet and my hands and I end up fidgeting the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling in your legs tend to happen more in the evening, or after you've been sitting for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really... I mean, I wouldn't say it's an evening thing specifically, or even that it only happens after I've been sitting for ages. It's not like that at all. It's more just... whenever I'm expected to sit, really. It could be first thing in the morning! We have our morning staff briefings at 8:00 AM sharp, and sometimes I've barely sat down with my tea, and within five minutes, I'm already feeling that sort of buzzing start up in my legs. I'll be sitting there, listening to the headmaster drone on about the new attendance policy or whatever it is that day, and I'm already shifting around, thinking about getting up to look at the notice board, even though I just looked at it on my way in. \n\nAnd then, of course, it happens in the evening too, like at those dinner parties I mentioned, or if my husband and I are just trying to sit and watch a program on the television after dinner. We love those historical dramas, you know, being a history teacher and all, but even then, I can't just sink into the sofa and stay there for an hour. I'll be up checking the kettle, or deciding I need to fold the laundry right that second. \n\nSo, it's not really tied to the time of day, and I don't have to be sitting for hours and hours for it to start. It's just the very act of having to stay seated when my body clearly wants to be doing something else. It's like my internal engine is just always idling a bit too high, whether it's breakfast time or supper time, whether I've been sitting for five minutes or fifty. It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!\n\nDoctor: Does the restless feeling in your legs get better when you stand up or move around?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does, in a way... but not like a magical cure or anything. The buzzing, the tapping, the need to shift. \n\nSo, yes, the moving around definitely helps while I'm doing it. If I could just stay in motion all day, I probably wouldn't even be here talking to you! But the problem is, society expects a 60-year-old woman to sit down eventually, doesn't it? And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness in your legs, is there ever any discomfort or an uncomfortable sensation—like aching, tingling, or crawling—that makes you want to move them?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. It’s not a... a physical discomfort, if you see what I mean. Not an ache, certainly not a tingling, and definitely not that awful crawling feeling you hear people talk about. I had a colleague once, a lovely woman who taught geography, who had something like that—she said it felt like spiders in her veins, which just sounds absolutely dreadful, doesn't it? But no, it’s nothing like that for me. \n\nIt’s really just... energy. Pure, unadulterated energy that simply has no place to go. It’s not painful in the slightest, just incredibly insistent. It’s as if my legs are saying, \"Right then, we're done sitting, let's get on with it!\" even if my brain is saying, \"Please, just let me finish marking this one last essay on the French Revolution.\" There’s no physical sensation of illness or ache, just this overwhelming, bubbling drive to be doing something else, somewhere else, preferably on my feet. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel this same restless energy in your legs when you're already standing or walking, or does it only happen when you're sitting down?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling in your legs tend to happen more in the evening, or after you've been sitting for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really... I mean, I wouldn't say it's an evening thing specifically, or even that it only happens after I've been sitting for ages. It's not like that at all. It's more just... whenever I'm expected to sit, really. It could be first thing in the morning! We have our morning staff briefings at 8:00 AM sharp, and sometimes I've barely sat down with my tea, and within five minutes, I'm already feeling that sort of buzzing start up in my legs. I'll be sitting there, listening to the headmaster drone on about the new attendance policy or whatever it is that day, and I'm already shifting around, thinking about getting up to look at the notice board, even though I just looked at it on my way in. \n\nAnd then, of course, it happens in the evening too, like at those dinner parties I mentioned, or if my husband and I are just trying to sit and watch a program on the television after dinner. We love those historical dramas, you know, being a history teacher and all, but even then, I can't just sink into the sofa and stay there for an hour. I'll be up checking the kettle, or deciding I need to fold the laundry right that second. \n\nSo, it's not really tied to the time of day, and I don't have to be sitting for hours and hours for it to start. It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!\n\nDoctor: Does the restless feeling in your legs get better when you stand up or move around?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does, in a way... but not like a magical cure or anything. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, let me think about that... no, no, I don't believe so. In fact, I'm quite certain I don't. When I'm already up and about, I feel perfectly fine. It’s... well, it's quite the opposite, actually! When I’m on my feet, that restless energy just sort of dissipates, because it’s finally being used for what it wants to do, if that makes sense. 

Take my weekends, for instance. My husband and I, we love to go on these long walks—we'll go down to the local historical society or just wander through the village, perhaps pop into an antique shop or two, and I could walk for absolute hours. My legs never feel restless or twitchy when we're doing that. Or at school, when I'm on break duty in the corridors. You can imagine the absolute chaos of it, teenagers everywhere, locker doors slamming, but I'm just pacing up and down, keeping an eye on things, making sure no one is loitering by the science labs, and I feel completely in my element. No buzzing, no urge to shake my leg, because, well, I'm already moving! 

It truly is just the sitting down that brings it on. It's the physical constraint of it, the expectation of being trapped in a chair. The moment my bottom hits a seat, the countdown begins. But if I'm already on my feet? Oh, I'm right as rain. I can stand at the front of the room—at the smartboard, they call it now, though I must admit I still miss the old chalkboards sometimes, there was something so satisfying about writing with proper chalk—anyway, I can stand there for a double period of A-level history, walking back and forth, and I won't even think about my legs once. So no, it’s never when I’m already walking or standing. It’s entirely a sitting-down affair.

I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Well, I suppose I should start by saying that I'm a secondary school teacher, so I am very used to being on my feet all day. I teach history, and I've always loved the energy of the classroom, pacing around, keeping the teenagers engaged. But what actually brought me in today is... well, it's becoming quite embarrassing, to be honest. I just keep getting up when I'm supposed to be sitting down. \n\nIt happens down at the school during our staff meetings, and even in my personal life, like at dinner parties when everyone else is perfectly content just remaining seated. I'll be halfway through a massive stack of papers on the Industrial Revolution, trying to focus on what my students have written, and I'll suddenly realize I'm just constantly in motion. \n\nI’ll be tapping my foot against the back of the desk, just tap-tap-tapping away, or I'll be clicking my red grading pen without even realizing I'm doing it. Click, click, click. And I'm shifting my weight from one hip to the other, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, adjusting my posture... it's exhausting! \n\nIt’s the exact same thing when I’m in church on a Sunday, which is terribly embarrassing. I’ll be sitting in the pew during the vicar's sermon, and I just squirm and shift around so much that I literally have to have a stern internal monologue and remind myself to just settle down. I’ll think, 'For goodness sake, you’re a grown woman, sit still!' But it’s just this restless energy that absolutely has to go somewhere, so if I can't stand up and walk it off like I want to, it just sort of leaks out into my feet and my hands and I end up fidgeting the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling in your legs tend to happen more in the evening, or after you've been sitting for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really... If I could just stay in motion all day, I probably wouldn't even be here talking to you! But the problem is, society expects a 60-year-old woman to sit down eventually, doesn't it? And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness in your legs, is there ever any discomfort or an uncomfortable sensation—like aching, tingling, or crawling—that makes you want to move them?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. It’s not a... a physical discomfort, if you see what I mean. Not an ache, certainly not a tingling, and definitely not that awful crawling feeling you hear people talk about. I had a colleague once, a lovely woman who taught geography, who had something like that—she said it felt like spiders in her veins, which just sounds absolutely dreadful, doesn't it? But no, it’s nothing like that for me. \n\nIt’s really just... energy. Pure, unadulterated energy that simply has no place to go. It’s not painful in the slightest, just incredibly insistent. It’s as if my legs are saying, \"Right then, we're done sitting, let's get on with it!\" even if my brain is saying, \"Please, just let me finish marking this one last essay on the French Revolution.\" There’s no physical sensation of illness or ache, just this overwhelming, bubbling drive to be doing something else, somewhere else, preferably on my feet. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this same restless energy in your legs when you're already standing or walking, or does it only happen when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... no, no, I don't believe so. In fact, I'm quite certain I don't. When I'm already up and about, I feel perfectly fine. It’s... well, it's quite the opposite, actually! When I’m on my feet, that restless energy just sort of dissipates, because it’s finally being used for what it wants to do, if that makes sense. \n\nTake my weekends, for instance. My husband and I, we love to go on these long walks—we'll go down to the local historical society or just wander through the village, perhaps pop into an antique shop or two, and I could walk for absolute hours. My legs never feel restless or twitchy when we're doing that. Or at school, when I'm on break duty in the corridors. You can imagine the absolute chaos of it, teenagers everywhere, locker doors slamming, but I'm just pacing up and down, keeping an eye on things, making sure no one is loitering by the science labs, and I feel completely in my element. No buzzing, no urge to shake my leg, because, well, I'm already moving! \n\nIt truly is just the sitting down that brings it on. It's the physical constraint of it, the expectation of being trapped in a chair. The moment my bottom hits a seat, the countdown begins. But if I'm already on my feet? Oh, I'm right as rain. I can stand at the front of the room—at the smartboard, they call it now, though I must admit I still miss the old chalkboards sometimes, there was something so satisfying about writing with proper chalk—anyway, I can stand there for a double period of A-level history, walking back and forth, and I won't even think about my legs once. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not really. I mean, well, let me think about that for a moment. When I'm trying to relax at night... no, I wouldn't say I do anything automatically with my legs then. It's actually quite the opposite, in a way. When I finally get into bed—which, I must admit, is often quite late because I always seem to find one more thing to do, one more essay to glance over, or I'll remember I need to set out my clothes for the next day, and then I'll decide my entire wardrobe needs reorganizing right then and there—but when I do finally lie down, I'm usually just exhausted. 

My husband, Arthur, he's out like a light the moment his head hits the pillow. It's truly astonishing. But me, my mind might still be racing, thinking about the lesson plan for the Year 11s or whether I was too harsh on that boy in the third row who wouldn't stop talking during the presentation on the industrial revolution. But my legs? No, they aren't moving on their own. 

I don't find myself kicking or twitching or anything like that when I'm trying to sleep. Once I'm horizontal, the physical need to move seems to settle down. It's really just during the day, when I'm sitting up, trying to focus on something stationary, like a staff meeting or marking papers. But at night, in bed, or if I'm, say, trying to watch a program on the television—though I rarely make it through a whole hour without getting up to put the kettle on or tidy the magazines—if I am actually just lying there trying to sleep, no, I don't notice any automatic movements. I suppose my body finally realizes the day is done, even if my brain hasn't quite caught up yet. So, no, nothing like that at night.

It’s more of a... a buzzing, I suppose? Or just this incredible restlessness that sort of builds up. And I'm shifting my weight from one hip to the other, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, adjusting my posture... it's exhausting! \n\nIt’s the exact same thing when I’m in church on a Sunday, which is terribly embarrassing. I’ll be sitting in the pew during the vicar's sermon, and I just squirm and shift around so much that I literally have to have a stern internal monologue and remind myself to just settle down. I’ll think, 'For goodness sake, you’re a grown woman, sit still!' But it’s just this restless energy that absolutely has to go somewhere, so if I can't stand up and walk it off like I want to, it just sort of leaks out into my feet and my hands and I end up fidgeting the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling in your legs tend to happen more in the evening, or after you've been sitting for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really... It's more just... whenever I'm expected to sit, really. It could be first thing in the morning! We have our morning staff briefings at 8:00 AM sharp, and sometimes I've barely sat down with my tea, and within five minutes, I'm already feeling that sort of buzzing start up in my legs. I'll be sitting there, listening to the headmaster drone on about the new attendance policy or whatever it is that day, and I'm already shifting around, thinking about getting up to look at the notice board, even though I just looked at it on my way in. \n\nAnd then, of course, it happens in the evening too, like at those dinner parties I mentioned, or if my husband and I are just trying to sit and watch a program on the television after dinner. It's like my internal engine is just always idling a bit too high, whether it's breakfast time or supper time, whether I've been sitting for five minutes or fifty. It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!\n\nDoctor: Does the restless feeling in your legs get better when you stand up or move around?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does, in a way... but not like a magical cure or anything. But the second I have to sit back down, say, if the students are taking an exam and I need to supervise from my desk... oh, it just starts right back up again. The buzzing, the tapping, the need to shift. \n\nSo, yes, the moving around definitely helps while I'm doing it. If I could just stay in motion all day, I probably wouldn't even be here talking to you! But the problem is, society expects a 60-year-old woman to sit down eventually, doesn't it? And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness in your legs, is there ever any discomfort or an uncomfortable sensation—like aching, tingling, or crawling—that makes you want to move them?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. It’s not a... a physical discomfort, if you see what I mean. Not an ache, certainly not a tingling, and definitely not that awful crawling feeling you hear people talk about. I had a colleague once, a lovely woman who taught geography, who had something like that—she said it felt like spiders in her veins, which just sounds absolutely dreadful, doesn't it? But no, it’s nothing like that for me. \n\nIt’s really just... energy. Pure, unadulterated energy that simply has no place to go. It’s not painful in the slightest, just incredibly insistent. It’s as if my legs are saying, \"Right then, we're done sitting, let's get on with it!\" even if my brain is saying, \"Please, just let me finish marking this one last essay on the French Revolution.\" There’s no physical sensation of illness or ache, just this overwhelming, bubbling drive to be doing something else, somewhere else, preferably on my feet. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this same restless energy in your legs when you're already standing or walking, or does it only happen when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... no, no, I don't believe so. In fact, I'm quite certain I don't. When I'm already up and about, I feel perfectly fine. It’s... well, it's quite the opposite, actually! When I’m on my feet, that restless energy just sort of dissipates, because it’s finally being used for what it wants to do, if that makes sense. \n\nTake my weekends, for instance. My husband and I, we love to go on these long walks—we'll go down to the local historical society or just wander through the village, perhaps pop into an antique shop or two, and I could walk for absolute hours. My legs never feel restless or twitchy when we're doing that. Or at school, when I'm on break duty in the corridors. You can imagine the absolute chaos of it, teenagers everywhere, locker doors slamming, but I'm just pacing up and down, keeping an eye on things, making sure no one is loitering by the science labs, and I feel completely in my element. No buzzing, no urge to shake my leg, because, well, I'm already moving! \n\nIt truly is just the sitting down that brings it on. It's the physical constraint of it, the expectation of being trapped in a chair. The moment my bottom hits a seat, the countdown begins. But if I'm already on my feet? Oh, I'm right as rain. I can stand at the front of the room—at the smartboard, they call it now, though I must admit I still miss the old chalkboards sometimes, there was something so satisfying about writing with proper chalk—anyway, I can stand there for a double period of A-level history, walking back and forth, and I won't even think about my legs once. So no, it’s never when I’m already walking or standing. It’s entirely a sitting-down affair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself moving your legs or doing anything else automatically when you're trying to relax or fall asleep at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, well, let me think about that for a moment. When I'm trying to relax at night... no, I wouldn't say I do anything automatically with my legs then. It's actually quite the opposite, in a way. When I finally get into bed—which, I must admit, is often quite late because I always seem to find one more thing to do, one more essay to glance over, or I'll remember I need to set out my clothes for the next day, and then I'll decide my entire wardrobe needs reorganizing right then and there—but when I do finally lie down, I'm usually just exhausted. \n\nMy husband, Arthur, he's out like a light the moment his head hits the pillow. It's truly astonishing. But me, my mind might still be racing, thinking about the lesson plan for the Year 11s or whether I was too harsh on that boy in the third row who wouldn't stop talking during the presentation on the industrial revolution. But my legs? No, they aren't moving on their own. \n\nI don't find myself kicking or twitching or anything like that when I'm trying to sleep. Once I'm horizontal, the physical need to move seems to settle down. It's really just during the day, when I'm sitting up, trying to focus on something stationary, like a staff meeting or marking papers. But at night, in bed, or if I'm, say, trying to watch a program on the television—though I rarely make it through a whole hour without getting up to put the kettle on or tidy the magazines—if I am actually just lying there trying to sleep, no, I don't notice any automatic movements. I suppose my body finally realizes the day is done, even if my brain hasn't quite caught up yet. So, no, nothing like that at night.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this same restless energy in your legs when you're already standing or walking, or does it only happen when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... no, no, I don't believe so. In fact, I'm quite certain I don't. When I'm already up and about, I feel perfectly fine. It’s... well, it's quite the opposite, actually! When I’m on my feet, that restless energy just sort of dissipates, because it’s finally being used for what it wants to do, if that makes sense. \n\nTake my weekends, for instance. My husband and I, we love to go on these long walks—we'll go down to the local historical society or just wander through the village, perhaps pop into an antique shop or two, and I could walk for absolute hours. My legs never feel restless or twitchy when we're doing that. Or at school, when I'm on break duty in the corridors. You can imagine the absolute chaos of it, teenagers everywhere, locker doors slamming, but I'm just pacing up and down, keeping an eye on things, making sure no one is loitering by the science labs, and I feel completely in my element. No buzzing, no urge to shake my leg, because, well, I'm already moving! \n\nIt truly is just the sitting down that brings it on. It's the physical constraint of it, the expectation of being trapped in a chair. The moment my bottom hits a seat, the countdown begins. But if I'm already on my feet? Oh, I'm right as rain. I can stand at the front of the room—at the smartboard, they call it now, though I must admit I still miss the old chalkboards sometimes, there was something so satisfying about writing with proper chalk—anyway, I can stand there for a double period of A-level history, walking back and forth, and I won't even think about my legs once. So no, it’s never when I’m already walking or standing. It’s entirely a sitting-down affair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself moving your legs or doing anything else automatically when you're trying to relax or fall asleep at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, well, let me think about that for a moment. When I'm trying to relax at night... no, I wouldn't say I do anything automatically with my legs then. It's actually quite the opposite, in a way. When I finally get into bed—which, I must admit, is often quite late because I always seem to find one more thing to do, one more essay to glance over, or I'll remember I need to set out my clothes for the next day, and then I'll decide my entire wardrobe needs reorganizing right then and there—but when I do finally lie down, I'm usually just exhausted. \n\nMy husband, Arthur, he's out like a light the moment his head hits the pillow. It's truly astonishing. But me, my mind might still be racing, thinking about the lesson plan for the Year 11s or whether I was too harsh on that boy in the third row who wouldn't stop talking during the presentation on the industrial revolution. But my legs? No, they aren't moving on their own. \n\nI don't find myself kicking or twitching or anything like that when I'm trying to sleep. Once I'm horizontal, the physical need to move seems to settle down. It's really just during the day, when I'm sitting up, trying to focus on something stationary, like a staff meeting or marking papers. But at night, in bed, or if I'm, say, trying to watch a program on the television—though I rarely make it through a whole hour without getting up to put the kettle on or tidy the magazines—if I am actually just lying there trying to sleep, no, I don't notice any automatic movements. I suppose my body finally realizes the day is done, even if my brain hasn't quite caught up yet. So, no, nothing like that at night.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever noticed that when you're first trying to fall asleep, you feel the urge to move your legs even though you're lying down?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, not an urge either. It really does tie back to what I was just saying, you see. Once I'm actually in the bed, lying flat, my legs seem to finally understand that the working day is over. They’ve done their pacing, they’ve walked the corridors, they’ve stood at the smartboard—so by the time I’m actually trying to fall asleep, they are perfectly content to just... stop. 

There’s no crawling, no twitching, and certainly no urge to get up and march about. It’s quite the contrast to my mind, really! I'll be lying there, perfectly still physically, but mentally I'm running a marathon. I'll be re-evaluating the entire Tudor syllabus or wondering if I remembered to lock the filing cabinet in the staff room. Just the other night, for instance, I spent what must have been forty-five minutes debating with myself whether I should switch from the history textbook we've been using for the last five years to that new one the exam board recommended. Arthur was snoring away beside me, completely oblivious, and I was wide awake, staring at the ceiling, going over chapter structures in my head. 

But through all of that mental gymnastics, my body itself is quite happy to remain stationary. I don't feel that bubbling need to move my legs like I do when I'm trapped in a chair during the day. It’s as if gravity does the trick, or perhaps it’s just the sheer exhaustion of being on my feet for so many hours. But no, no urge to move them at all once I'm lying down to sleep. They just feel heavy and tired, which is exactly how they ought to feel at that hour, I suppose!

I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Well, I suppose I should start by saying that I'm a secondary school teacher, so I am very used to being on my feet all day. I teach history, and I've always loved the energy of the classroom, pacing around, keeping the teenagers engaged. But what actually brought me in today is... well, it's becoming quite embarrassing, to be honest. I just keep getting up when I'm supposed to be sitting down. \n\nIt happens down at the school during our staff meetings, and even in my personal life, like at dinner parties when everyone else is perfectly content just remaining seated. I’ll be sitting there, and suddenly I just feel this need to move, so I'll invent a reason to get up. I'll pop up to refill my coffee—even if my cup is still half full, which is ridiculous—or I'll suddenly decide I need to go check a handout on the other side of the room. Or sometimes I just walk to the back of the room and stand there for a while! And the thing is, none of it is ever really urgent. \n\nMy husband actually pointed it out at a dinner last week. We were at our friends' house, we had just finished the main course, and I just stood up to go look at a painting on their wall. I mean, who does that? Everyone else is sitting, having a lovely conversation, and I'm wandering around their dining room. It's just... I don't know, I'm 60 years old, people expect me to be able to sit through a normal, quiet event, but it's like my legs just decide it's time to go and I can't stay in my chair without finding some excuse to be on my feet.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you’re feeling in your legs when you get the urge to stand up? Is it more of a restlessness, discomfort, or something else that makes it hard to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s certainly not a pain or a physical ache, if that’s what you mean. It’s more of a... a buzzing, I suppose? Or just this incredible restlessness that sort of builds up. If I try to ignore it and just force myself to stay in the chair—because, as I said, sometimes you really just can't get up, no matter how much you want to. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays. Oh, the essays. I'll be halfway through a massive stack of papers on the Industrial Revolution, trying to focus on what my students have written, and I'll suddenly realize I'm just constantly in motion. \n\nI’ll be tapping my foot against the back of the desk, just tap-tap-tapping away, or I'll be clicking my red grading pen without even realizing I'm doing it. Click, click, click. And I'm shifting my weight from one hip to the other, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, adjusting my posture... it's exhausting! \n\nIt’s the exact same thing when I’m in church on a Sunday, which is terribly embarrassing. I’ll be sitting in the pew during the vicar's sermon, and I just squirm and shift around so much that I literally have to have a stern internal monologue and remind myself to just settle down. I’ll think, 'For goodness sake, you’re a grown woman, sit still!' But it’s just this restless energy that absolutely has to go somewhere, so if I can't stand up and walk it off like I want to, it just sort of leaks out into my feet and my hands and I end up fidgeting the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling in your legs tend to happen more in the evening, or after you've been sitting for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really... I mean, I wouldn't say it's an evening thing specifically, or even that it only happens after I've been sitting for ages. It's not like that at all. It's more just... whenever I'm expected to sit, really. It could be first thing in the morning! We have our morning staff briefings at 8:00 AM sharp, and sometimes I've barely sat down with my tea, and within five minutes, I'm already feeling that sort of buzzing start up in my legs. I'll be sitting there, listening to the headmaster drone on about the new attendance policy or whatever it is that day, and I'm already shifting around, thinking about getting up to look at the notice board, even though I just looked at it on my way in. \n\nAnd then, of course, it happens in the evening too, like at those dinner parties I mentioned, or if my husband and I are just trying to sit and watch a program on the television after dinner. We love those historical dramas, you know, being a history teacher and all, but even then, I can't just sink into the sofa and stay there for an hour. I'll be up checking the kettle, or deciding I need to fold the laundry right that second. \n\nSo, it's not really tied to the time of day, and I don't have to be sitting for hours and hours for it to start. It's just the very act of having to stay seated when my body clearly wants to be doing something else. It's like my internal engine is just always idling a bit too high, whether it's breakfast time or supper time, whether I've been sitting for five minutes or fifty. It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!\n\nDoctor: Does the restless feeling in your legs get better when you stand up or move around?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does, in a way... but not like a magical cure or anything. It’s more that when I finally do get up—like when I invent that excuse to go check the coffee pot or wander over to the notice board during the staff briefing—it’s as if a pressure valve has been released, just for a moment. But it’s not as if the restlessness completely disappears forever just because I took a little walk across the room. \n\nI mean, yes, while I'm actually walking, while I'm moving, I feel much more... well, normal, I suppose. I feel like myself. When I’m pacing at the front of my classroom, lecturing about the Tudor dynasty, I feel absolutely fine! I don't even think about my legs. But the second I have to sit back down, say, if the students are taking an exam and I need to supervise from my desk... oh, it just starts right back up again. The buzzing, the tapping, the need to shift. \n\nSo, yes, the moving around definitely helps while I'm doing it. If I could just stay in motion all day, I probably wouldn't even be here talking to you! But the problem is, society expects a 60-year-old woman to sit down eventually, doesn't it? And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness in your legs, is there ever any discomfort or an uncomfortable sensation—like aching, tingling, or crawling—that makes you want to move them?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. It’s not a... a physical discomfort, if you see what I mean. Not an ache, certainly not a tingling, and definitely not that awful crawling feeling you hear people talk about. I had a colleague once, a lovely woman who taught geography, who had something like that—she said it felt like spiders in her veins, which just sounds absolutely dreadful, doesn't it? But no, it’s nothing like that for me. \n\nIt’s really just... energy. Pure, unadulterated energy that simply has no place to go. It’s not painful in the slightest, just incredibly insistent. It’s as if my legs are saying, \"Right then, we're done sitting, let's get on with it!\" even if my brain is saying, \"Please, just let me finish marking this one last essay on the French Revolution.\" There’s no physical sensation of illness or ache, just this overwhelming, bubbling drive to be doing something else, somewhere else, preferably on my feet. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this same restless energy in your legs when you're already standing or walking, or does it only happen when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... no, no, I don't believe so. It’s as if my legs are saying, \"Right then, we're done sitting, let's get on with it!\" even if my brain is saying, \"Please, just let me finish marking this one last essay on the French Revolution.\" There’s no physical sensation of illness or ache, just this overwhelming, bubbling drive to be doing something else, somewhere else, preferably on my feet. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this same restless energy in your legs when you're already standing or walking, or does it only happen when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... no, no, I don't believe so. In fact, I'm quite certain I don't. When I'm already up and about, I feel perfectly fine. It’s... well, it's quite the opposite, actually! When I’m on my feet, that restless energy just sort of dissipates, because it’s finally being used for what it wants to do, if that makes sense. \n\nTake my weekends, for instance. My husband and I, we love to go on these long walks—we'll go down to the local historical society or just wander through the village, perhaps pop into an antique shop or two, and I could walk for absolute hours. My legs never feel restless or twitchy when we're doing that. Or at school, when I'm on break duty in the corridors. You can imagine the absolute chaos of it, teenagers everywhere, locker doors slamming, but I'm just pacing up and down, keeping an eye on things, making sure no one is loitering by the science labs, and I feel completely in my element. No buzzing, no urge to shake my leg, because, well, I'm already moving! \n\nIt truly is just the sitting down that brings it on. It's the physical constraint of it, the expectation of being trapped in a chair. The moment my bottom hits a seat, the countdown begins. But if I'm already on my feet? Oh, I'm right as rain. I can stand at the front of the room—at the smartboard, they call it now, though I must admit I still miss the old chalkboards sometimes, there was something so satisfying about writing with proper chalk—anyway, I can stand there for a double period of A-level history, walking back and forth, and I won't even think about my legs once. So no, it’s never when I’m already walking or standing. It’s entirely a sitting-down affair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself moving your legs or doing anything else automatically when you're trying to relax or fall asleep at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, well, let me think about that for a moment. When I'm trying to relax at night... no, I wouldn't say I do anything automatically with my legs then. It's actually quite the opposite, in a way. When I finally get into bed—which, I must admit, is often quite late because I always seem to find one more thing to do, one more essay to glance over, or I'll remember I need to set out my clothes for the next day, and then I'll decide my entire wardrobe needs reorganizing right then and there—but when I do finally lie down, I'm usually just exhausted. \n\nMy husband, Arthur, he's out like a light the moment his head hits the pillow. It's truly astonishing. But me, my mind might still be racing, thinking about the lesson plan for the Year 11s or whether I was too harsh on that boy in the third row who wouldn't stop talking during the presentation on the industrial revolution. But my legs? No, they aren't moving on their own. \n\nI don't find myself kicking or twitching or anything like that when I'm trying to sleep. Once I'm horizontal, the physical need to move seems to settle down. It's really just during the day, when I'm sitting up, trying to focus on something stationary, like a staff meeting or marking papers. But at night, in bed, or if I'm, say, trying to watch a program on the television—though I rarely make it through a whole hour without getting up to put the kettle on or tidy the magazines—if I am actually just lying there trying to sleep, no, I don't notice any automatic movements. I suppose my body finally realizes the day is done, even if my brain hasn't quite caught up yet. So, no, nothing like that at night.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed that when you're first trying to fall asleep, you feel the urge to move your legs even though you're lying down?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not an urge either. It really does tie back to what I was just saying, you see. Once I'm actually in the bed, lying flat, my legs seem to finally understand that the working day is over. They’ve done their pacing, they’ve walked the corridors, they’ve stood at the smartboard—so by the time I’m actually trying to fall asleep, they are perfectly content to just... stop. \n\nThere’s no crawling, no twitching, and certainly no urge to get up and march about. It’s quite the contrast to my mind, really! I'll be lying there, perfectly still physically, but mentally I'm running a marathon. I'll be re-evaluating the entire Tudor syllabus or wondering if I remembered to lock the filing cabinet in the staff room. Just the other night, for instance, I spent what must have been forty-five minutes debating with myself whether I should switch from the history textbook we've been using for the last five years to that new one the exam board recommended. Arthur was snoring away beside me, completely oblivious, and I was wide awake, staring at the ceiling, going over chapter structures in my head. \n\nBut through all of that mental gymnastics, my body itself is quite happy to remain stationary. I don't feel that bubbling need to move my legs like I do when I'm trapped in a chair during the day. It’s as if gravity does the trick, or perhaps it’s just the sheer exhaustion of being on my feet for so many hours. But no, no urge to move them at all once I'm lying down to sleep. They just feel heavy and tired, which is exactly how they ought to feel at that hour, I suppose!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, I wouldn't say it's worse in the evening, no. It really just comes down to the sitting itself, regardless of what the clock on the wall says. I mean, if I'm sitting in a staff briefing at half-past eight in the morning, listening to the headmaster go on and on about the new attendance policy—which, frankly, is nearly identical to the old attendance policy, just with considerably more paperwork for us teachers—my legs are just as restless, just as desperate to get moving, as they are at eight o'clock at night. 

It is true that I do a fair bit of sitting in the evenings, of course, which might make it seem that way. After dinner, Arthur and I usually move into the sitting room. He'll have his newspaper or perhaps a crossword, and I'll invariably have a massive stack of marking on my lap. It's mock exam season for the GCSE students right now, so you can imagine the sheer volume of essays I'm wading through on the causes of the First World War. And yes, when I sit down on the sofa with my red pen, that familiar buzzing feeling starts up almost immediately, and I'll find myself bouncing my heel or needing to shift my position every few minutes. 

But is it actually worse than during the day? No, I don't believe so. I think it's just that the evening is when I'm expected to sit still for longer, uninterrupted stretches, isn't it? During the school day, if I get that restless feeling while I'm sitting at my desk, I can just pop up and walk around the classroom, check on the students' progress, or nip down to the staff room to make a quick cup of tea. But in the evening, when we're trying to watch a bit of television—we've been watching a new historical drama lately, though I must say their attention to period-accurate costumes is shockingly poor, they had a woman in a 1920s scene wearing a zipper that clearly wasn't invented until much later—but anyway, when I'm supposed to be just relaxing on the sofa, the restlessness is perhaps more noticeable to me. I'm trying so hard to just sit and be still like a normal person relaxing, and I simply can't. 

So the actual physical feeling in my legs? No, it's exactly the same intensity as it is at ten in the morning. It's just the act of being stuck in a chair that does it, morning, noon, or night.
