description: 8 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably harder to manage for about eight months, since my youngest left for college and the house became quieter.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 60 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you notice that the restlessness in your legs tends to be worse during the evening hours compared to other times of the day, especially when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's worse in the evening, no. It really just comes down to the sitting itself, regardless of what the clock on the wall says. I mean, if I'm sitting in a staff briefing at half-past eight in the morning, listening to the headmaster go on and on about the new attendance policy—which, frankly, is nearly identical to the old attendance policy, just with considerably more paperwork for us teachers—my legs are just as restless, just as desperate to get moving, as they are at eight o'clock at night. \n\nIt is true that I do a fair bit of sitting in the evenings, of course, which might make it seem that way. After dinner, Arthur and I usually move into the sitting room. He'll have his newspaper or perhaps a crossword, and I'll invariably have a massive stack of marking on my lap. It's mock exam season for the GCSE students right now, so you can imagine the sheer volume of essays I'm wading through on the causes of the First World War. And yes, when I sit down on the sofa with my red pen, that familiar buzzing feeling starts up almost immediately, and I'll find myself bouncing my heel or needing to shift my position every few minutes. \n\nBut is it actually worse than during the day? No, I don't believe so. I think it's just that the evening is when I'm expected to sit still for longer, uninterrupted stretches, isn't it? During the school day, if I get that restless feeling while I'm sitting at my desk, I can just pop up and walk around the classroom, check on the students' progress, or nip down to the staff room to make a quick cup of tea. But in the evening, when we're trying to watch a bit of television—we've been watching a new historical drama lately, though I must say their attention to period-accurate costumes is shockingly poor, they had a woman in a 1920s scene wearing a zipper that clearly wasn't invented until much later—but anyway, when I'm supposed to be just relaxing on the sofa, the restlessness is perhaps more noticeable to me. I'm trying so hard to just sit and be still like a normal person relaxing, and I simply can't. \n\nSo the actual physical feeling in my legs? No, it's exactly the same intensity as it is at ten in the morning. It's just the act of being stuck in a chair that does it, morning, noon, or night.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that your legs jerk or move suddenly while you're falling asleep or during the night?\n\nPatient: No, no one has ever mentioned anything of the sort. And by no one, I really mean Arthur, of course, since he’s the only one who would be in a position to know! \n\nNow, as I mentioned earlier, Arthur is an incredibly heavy sleeper. He really is. He could probably sleep through a brass band parading through our bedroom, or at the very least, a moderately sized earthquake. But I am quite certain that if I were actually kicking him or jerking my legs about violently in the middle of the night, he’d eventually notice and say something. He’s rather particular about his sleep environment, you see. If I so much as turn the page of my book too loudly—because I do often have to read to try and quiet my mind, usually a historical biography or something quite dense, just to tire my eyes out—he’ll grumble and pull the duvet over his ears. \n\nSo a physical kick? Or a sudden jerk? Oh, I would absolutely hear about that the next morning over breakfast. He’d be terribly grumpy about it while buttering his toast, I assure you, and he wouldn't let me forget it. He'd probably suggest we get separate blankets like they do in Scandinavia—I read an article about that recently, actually, how it saves marriages, though I think it looks terribly untidy when you make the bed. \n\nBut no, he has never once complained about me twitching or jerking in my sleep. And I’ve never woken myself up doing it, either. I really do think that once I'm finally horizontal and the lights are out, my body is just completely still. It's almost a relief to my legs, I suppose. Even if my brain is still rattling on, drafting lesson plans or worrying about the state of the national curriculum, my limbs are perfectly quiet. So, no, no sudden movements or jerking at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this restless feeling in your legs even when you're not required to sit still, such as when you're at home doing chores or engaging in an activity you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not at all. If I'm up and about, doing chores or what have you, I don't feel that restlessness in the slightest. It’s really quite the opposite, actually! When I’m moving, I feel entirely... well, normal, I suppose. \n\nTake the weekends, for example. Saturday mornings are my time for getting the house in order, and I am constantly on my feet. I’ll be up and down the stairs with the laundry, hoovering the sitting room, and then there’s the ironing. Arthur insists on wearing these dreadful heavy cotton shirts that seem to wrinkle if you so much as look at them crossly, so I can easily spend a good hour or more just stood at the ironing board. And during all of that, my legs are perfectly fine. There’s no buzzing, no urge to jiggle or shift about, because, well, I’m already moving, aren't I? \n\nIt’s exactly the same with things I actually enjoy doing, too. I’m quite a keen baker—I find it terribly relaxing to follow a recipe, it’s all so wonderfully precise, quite unlike marking Year 9 essays where you have to try and decipher what on earth they actually meant to say about the industrial revolution. So if I’m in the kitchen on a Sunday afternoon, measuring out flour for a Victoria sponge, bustling between the counter and the oven, I don’t feel any of that dreadful trapped feeling in my legs. Or if I'm out in the garden pruning the roses, for instance. I can spend hours out there, pottering about from the flowerbeds to the shed, and my body is perfectly content. \n\nIt really does just come back to the sitting, you see. If I’m allowed to be in motion, to be doing something physical, whether it’s wiping down the whiteboards at the end of the school day or just dusting the mantelpiece at home, the restlessness completely vanishes. It’s as if my body just wants to be put to use, and as long as I’m obliging it, it doesn’t give me a spot of bother. So no, I certainly don't get that feeling when I'm active or engaged in my chores. It's only when I'm forced to stop!\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say the urge to move your legs is something you feel you absolutely must act on, even if you know it might seem unnecessary or inconvenient?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, I wouldn't say 'absolutely must', no. It’s not as if my legs have a mind of their own and will just leap up and start tap-dancing without my permission! It’s more of a... a very strong preference, I suppose you could say. 

I mean, if I'm in a situation where it would be completely inappropriate or highly inconvenient to move, I *can* force myself to stay still. Take exam invigilation, for example. I mentioned the mock GCSEs earlier. When you're invigilating, you have to sit at that little desk at the front of the sports hall for up to two hours in absolute, pin-drop silence. The acoustics in our school hall are dreadful, by the way, if you so much as drop a biro it sounds like a gunshot. But anyway, when I’m sitting there, I do get that familiar buzzing, that desperate feeling of wanting to stretch or pace up and down the aisles. But I don't *absolutely* act on it, because I know it would distract the students. I don't just stand up and start doing star jumps in the middle of a history paper! 

Instead, I’ll find little ways to manage it that aren't quite so obvious. I might wiggle my toes furiously inside my shoes—I swear by Clarks, by the way, they have excellent arch support, which is absolutely vital when you've been teaching as long as I have and the school insists on those terribly hard linoleum floors. Or I might just sort of tense and relax my calf muscles under the desk where no one can see, or subtly bounce my heel. 

So, I can suppress it if the situation demands it. It just takes a rather exhausting amount of mental effort to do so. It feels a bit like holding a beach ball underwater, if that makes sense? You can do it, but you're constantly fighting the pressure of it wanting to pop back up, and the moment you stop paying attention, up it comes. But an uncontrollable urge that I simply *must* act on regardless of the consequences? No, no, I wouldn't say it's quite like that. I do still have some manners, after all!

========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nG21.3, F43.1, F43.2, F44.4, F44.5, F54\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you for seeing me. Well, I suppose I should start by saying that I'm a secondary school teacher, so I am very used to being on my feet all day. I teach history, and I've always loved the energy of the classroom, pacing around, keeping the teenagers engaged. But what actually brought me in today is... well, it's becoming quite embarrassing, to be honest. I just keep getting up when I'm supposed to be sitting down. \n\nIt happens down at the school during our staff meetings, and even in my personal life, like at dinner parties when everyone else is perfectly content just remaining seated. I’ll be sitting there, and suddenly I just feel this need to move, so I'll invent a reason to get up. I'll pop up to refill my coffee—even if my cup is still half full, which is ridiculous—or I'll suddenly decide I need to go check a handout on the other side of the room. Or sometimes I just walk to the back of the room and stand there for a while! And the thing is, none of it is ever really urgent. \n\nMy husband actually pointed it out at a dinner last week. We were at our friends' house, we had just finished the main course, and I just stood up to go look at a painting on their wall. I mean, who does that? Everyone else is sitting, having a lovely conversation, and I'm wandering around their dining room. It's just... I don't know, I'm 60 years old, people expect me to be able to sit through a normal, quiet event, but it's like my legs just decide it's time to go and I can't stay in my chair without finding some excuse to be on my feet.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you’re feeling in your legs when you get the urge to stand up? Is it more of a restlessness, discomfort, or something else that makes it hard to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s certainly not a pain or a physical ache, if that’s what you mean. It’s more of a... a buzzing, I suppose? Or just this incredible restlessness that sort of builds up. If I try to ignore it and just force myself to stay in the chair—because, as I said, sometimes you really just can't get up, no matter how much you want to. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays. Oh, the essays. I'll be halfway through a massive stack of papers on the Industrial Revolution, trying to focus on what my students have written, and I'll suddenly realize I'm just constantly in motion. \n\nI’ll be tapping my foot against the back of the desk, just tap-tap-tapping away, or I'll be clicking my red grading pen without even realizing I'm doing it. Click, click, click. And I'm shifting my weight from one hip to the other, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, adjusting my posture... it's exhausting! \n\nIt’s the exact same thing when I’m in church on a Sunday, which is terribly embarrassing. I’ll be sitting in the pew during the vicar's sermon, and I just squirm and shift around so much that I literally have to have a stern internal monologue and remind myself to just settle down. I’ll think, 'For goodness sake, you’re a grown woman, sit still!' But it’s just this restless energy that absolutely has to go somewhere, so if I can't stand up and walk it off like I want to, it just sort of leaks out into my feet and my hands and I end up fidgeting the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling in your legs tend to happen more in the evening, or after you've been sitting for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really... I mean, I wouldn't say it's an evening thing specifically, or even that it only happens after I've been sitting for ages. It's not like that at all. It's more just... whenever I'm expected to sit, really. It could be first thing in the morning! We have our morning staff briefings at 8:00 AM sharp, and sometimes I've barely sat down with my tea, and within five minutes, I'm already feeling that sort of buzzing start up in my legs. I'll be sitting there, listening to the headmaster drone on about the new attendance policy or whatever it is that day, and I'm already shifting around, thinking about getting up to look at the notice board, even though I just looked at it on my way in. \n\nAnd then, of course, it happens in the evening too, like at those dinner parties I mentioned, or if my husband and I are just trying to sit and watch a program on the television after dinner. We love those historical dramas, you know, being a history teacher and all, but even then, I can't just sink into the sofa and stay there for an hour. I'll be up checking the kettle, or deciding I need to fold the laundry right that second. \n\nSo, it's not really tied to the time of day, and I don't have to be sitting for hours and hours for it to start. It's just the very act of having to stay seated when my body clearly wants to be doing something else. It's like my internal engine is just always idling a bit too high, whether it's breakfast time or supper time, whether I've been sitting for five minutes or fifty. It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!\n\nDoctor: Does the restless feeling in your legs get better when you stand up or move around?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does, in a way... but not like a magical cure or anything. It’s more that when I finally do get up—like when I invent that excuse to go check the coffee pot or wander over to the notice board during the staff briefing—it’s as if a pressure valve has been released, just for a moment. But it’s not as if the restlessness completely disappears forever just because I took a little walk across the room. \n\nI mean, yes, while I'm actually walking, while I'm moving, I feel much more... well, normal, I suppose. I feel like myself. When I’m pacing at the front of my classroom, lecturing about the Tudor dynasty, I feel absolutely fine! I don't even think about my legs. But the second I have to sit back down, say, if the students are taking an exam and I need to supervise from my desk... oh, it just starts right back up again. The buzzing, the tapping, the need to shift. \n\nSo, yes, the moving around definitely helps while I'm doing it. If I could just stay in motion all day, I probably wouldn't even be here talking to you! But the problem is, society expects a 60-year-old woman to sit down eventually, doesn't it? And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness in your legs, is there ever any discomfort or an uncomfortable sensation—like aching, tingling, or crawling—that makes you want to move them?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. It’s not a... a physical discomfort, if you see what I mean. Not an ache, certainly not a tingling, and definitely not that awful crawling feeling you hear people talk about. I had a colleague once, a lovely woman who taught geography, who had something like that—she said it felt like spiders in her veins, which just sounds absolutely dreadful, doesn't it? But no, it’s nothing like that for me. \n\nIt’s really just... energy. Pure, unadulterated energy that simply has no place to go. It’s not painful in the slightest, just incredibly insistent. It’s as if my legs are saying, \"Right then, we're done sitting, let's get on with it!\" even if my brain is saying, \"Please, just let me finish marking this one last essay on the French Revolution.\" There’s no physical sensation of illness or ache, just this overwhelming, bubbling drive to be doing something else, somewhere else, preferably on my feet. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this same restless energy in your legs when you're already standing or walking, or does it only happen when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... no, no, I don't believe so. In fact, I'm quite certain I don't. When I'm already up and about, I feel perfectly fine. It’s... well, it's quite the opposite, actually! When I’m on my feet, that restless energy just sort of dissipates, because it’s finally being used for what it wants to do, if that makes sense. \n\nTake my weekends, for instance. My husband and I, we love to go on these long walks—we'll go down to the local historical society or just wander through the village, perhaps pop into an antique shop or two, and I could walk for absolute hours. My legs never feel restless or twitchy when we're doing that. Or at school, when I'm on break duty in the corridors. You can imagine the absolute chaos of it, teenagers everywhere, locker doors slamming, but I'm just pacing up and down, keeping an eye on things, making sure no one is loitering by the science labs, and I feel completely in my element. No buzzing, no urge to shake my leg, because, well, I'm already moving! \n\nIt truly is just the sitting down that brings it on. It's the physical constraint of it, the expectation of being trapped in a chair. The moment my bottom hits a seat, the countdown begins. But if I'm already on my feet? Oh, I'm right as rain. I can stand at the front of the room—at the smartboard, they call it now, though I must admit I still miss the old chalkboards sometimes, there was something so satisfying about writing with proper chalk—anyway, I can stand there for a double period of A-level history, walking back and forth, and I won't even think about my legs once. So no, it’s never when I’m already walking or standing. It’s entirely a sitting-down affair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself moving your legs or doing anything else automatically when you're trying to relax or fall asleep at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, well, let me think about that for a moment. When I'm trying to relax at night... no, I wouldn't say I do anything automatically with my legs then. It's actually quite the opposite, in a way. When I finally get into bed—which, I must admit, is often quite late because I always seem to find one more thing to do, one more essay to glance over, or I'll remember I need to set out my clothes for the next day, and then I'll decide my entire wardrobe needs reorganizing right then and there—but when I do finally lie down, I'm usually just exhausted. \n\nMy husband, Arthur, he's out like a light the moment his head hits the pillow. It's truly astonishing. But me, my mind might still be racing, thinking about the lesson plan for the Year 11s or whether I was too harsh on that boy in the third row who wouldn't stop talking during the presentation on the industrial revolution. But my legs? No, they aren't moving on their own. \n\nI don't find myself kicking or twitching or anything like that when I'm trying to sleep. Once I'm horizontal, the physical need to move seems to settle down. It's really just during the day, when I'm sitting up, trying to focus on something stationary, like a staff meeting or marking papers. But at night, in bed, or if I'm, say, trying to watch a program on the television—though I rarely make it through a whole hour without getting up to put the kettle on or tidy the magazines—if I am actually just lying there trying to sleep, no, I don't notice any automatic movements. I suppose my body finally realizes the day is done, even if my brain hasn't quite caught up yet. So, no, nothing like that at night.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed that when you're first trying to fall asleep, you feel the urge to move your legs even though you're lying down?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not an urge either. It really does tie back to what I was just saying, you see. Once I'm actually in the bed, lying flat, my legs seem to finally understand that the working day is over. They’ve done their pacing, they’ve walked the corridors, they’ve stood at the smartboard—so by the time I’m actually trying to fall asleep, they are perfectly content to just... stop. \n\nThere’s no crawling, no twitching, and certainly no urge to get up and march about. It’s quite the contrast to my mind, really! I'll be lying there, perfectly still physically, but mentally I'm running a marathon. I'll be re-evaluating the entire Tudor syllabus or wondering if I remembered to lock the filing cabinet in the staff room. Just the other night, for instance, I spent what must have been forty-five minutes debating with myself whether I should switch from the history textbook we've been using for the last five years to that new one the exam board recommended. Arthur was snoring away beside me, completely oblivious, and I was wide awake, staring at the ceiling, going over chapter structures in my head. \n\nBut through all of that mental gymnastics, my body itself is quite happy to remain stationary. I don't feel that bubbling need to move my legs like I do when I'm trapped in a chair during the day. It’s as if gravity does the trick, or perhaps it’s just the sheer exhaustion of being on my feet for so many hours. But no, no urge to move them at all once I'm lying down to sleep. They just feel heavy and tired, which is exactly how they ought to feel at that hour, I suppose!\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that the restlessness in your legs tends to be worse during the evening hours compared to other times of the day, especially when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's worse in the evening, no. It really just comes down to the sitting itself, regardless of what the clock on the wall says. I mean, if I'm sitting in a staff briefing at half-past eight in the morning, listening to the headmaster go on and on about the new attendance policy—which, frankly, is nearly identical to the old attendance policy, just with considerably more paperwork for us teachers—my legs are just as restless, just as desperate to get moving, as they are at eight o'clock at night. \n\nIt is true that I do a fair bit of sitting in the evenings, of course, which might make it seem that way. After dinner, Arthur and I usually move into the sitting room. He'll have his newspaper or perhaps a crossword, and I'll invariably have a massive stack of marking on my lap. It's mock exam season for the GCSE students right now, so you can imagine the sheer volume of essays I'm wading through on the causes of the First World War. And yes, when I sit down on the sofa with my red pen, that familiar buzzing feeling starts up almost immediately, and I'll find myself bouncing my heel or needing to shift my position every few minutes. \n\nBut is it actually worse than during the day? No, I don't believe so. I think it's just that the evening is when I'm expected to sit still for longer, uninterrupted stretches, isn't it? During the school day, if I get that restless feeling while I'm sitting at my desk, I can just pop up and walk around the classroom, check on the students' progress, or nip down to the staff room to make a quick cup of tea. But in the evening, when we're trying to watch a bit of television—we've been watching a new historical drama lately, though I must say their attention to period-accurate costumes is shockingly poor, they had a woman in a 1920s scene wearing a zipper that clearly wasn't invented until much later—but anyway, when I'm supposed to be just relaxing on the sofa, the restlessness is perhaps more noticeable to me. I'm trying so hard to just sit and be still like a normal person relaxing, and I simply can't. \n\nSo the actual physical feeling in my legs? No, it's exactly the same intensity as it is at ten in the morning. It's just the act of being stuck in a chair that does it, morning, noon, or night.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that your legs jerk or move suddenly while you're falling asleep or during the night?\n\nPatient: No, no one has ever mentioned anything of the sort. And by no one, I really mean Arthur, of course, since he’s the only one who would be in a position to know! \n\nNow, as I mentioned earlier, Arthur is an incredibly heavy sleeper. He really is. He could probably sleep through a brass band parading through our bedroom, or at the very least, a moderately sized earthquake. But I am quite certain that if I were actually kicking him or jerking my legs about violently in the middle of the night, he’d eventually notice and say something. He’s rather particular about his sleep environment, you see. If I so much as turn the page of my book too loudly—because I do often have to read to try and quiet my mind, usually a historical biography or something quite dense, just to tire my eyes out—he’ll grumble and pull the duvet over his ears. \n\nSo a physical kick? Or a sudden jerk? Oh, I would absolutely hear about that the next morning over breakfast. He’d be terribly grumpy about it while buttering his toast, I assure you, and he wouldn't let me forget it. He'd probably suggest we get separate blankets like they do in Scandinavia—I read an article about that recently, actually, how it saves marriages, though I think it looks terribly untidy when you make the bed. \n\nBut no, he has never once complained about me twitching or jerking in my sleep. And I’ve never woken myself up doing it, either. I really do think that once I'm finally horizontal and the lights are out, my body is just completely still. It's almost a relief to my legs, I suppose. Even if my brain is still rattling on, drafting lesson plans or worrying about the state of the national curriculum, my limbs are perfectly quiet. So, no, no sudden movements or jerking at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this restless feeling in your legs even when you're not required to sit still, such as when you're at home doing chores or engaging in an activity you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not at all. If I'm up and about, doing chores or what have you, I don't feel that restlessness in the slightest. It’s really quite the opposite, actually! When I’m moving, I feel entirely... well, normal, I suppose. \n\nTake the weekends, for example. Saturday mornings are my time for getting the house in order, and I am constantly on my feet. I’ll be up and down the stairs with the laundry, hoovering the sitting room, and then there’s the ironing. Arthur insists on wearing these dreadful heavy cotton shirts that seem to wrinkle if you so much as look at them crossly, so I can easily spend a good hour or more just stood at the ironing board. And during all of that, my legs are perfectly fine. There’s no buzzing, no urge to jiggle or shift about, because, well, I’m already moving, aren't I? \n\nIt’s exactly the same with things I actually enjoy doing, too. I’m quite a keen baker—I find it terribly relaxing to follow a recipe, it’s all so wonderfully precise, quite unlike marking Year 9 essays where you have to try and decipher what on earth they actually meant to say about the industrial revolution. So if I’m in the kitchen on a Sunday afternoon, measuring out flour for a Victoria sponge, bustling between the counter and the oven, I don’t feel any of that dreadful trapped feeling in my legs. Or if I'm out in the garden pruning the roses, for instance. I can spend hours out there, pottering about from the flowerbeds to the shed, and my body is perfectly content. \n\nIt really does just come back to the sitting, you see. If I’m allowed to be in motion, to be doing something physical, whether it’s wiping down the whiteboards at the end of the school day or just dusting the mantelpiece at home, the restlessness completely vanishes. It’s as if my body just wants to be put to use, and as long as I’m obliging it, it doesn’t give me a spot of bother. So no, I certainly don't get that feeling when I'm active or engaged in my chores. It's only when I'm forced to stop!\n\nDoctor: Would you say the urge to move your legs is something you feel you absolutely must act on, even if you know it might seem unnecessary or inconvenient?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say 'absolutely must', no. It’s not as if my legs have a mind of their own and will just leap up and start tap-dancing without my permission! It’s more of a... a very strong preference, I suppose you could say. \n\nI mean, if I'm in a situation where it would be completely inappropriate or highly inconvenient to move, I *can* force myself to stay still. Take exam invigilation, for example. I mentioned the mock GCSEs earlier. When you're invigilating, you have to sit at that little desk at the front of the sports hall for up to two hours in absolute, pin-drop silence. The acoustics in our school hall are dreadful, by the way, if you so much as drop a biro it sounds like a gunshot. But anyway, when I’m sitting there, I do get that familiar buzzing, that desperate feeling of wanting to stretch or pace up and down the aisles. But I don't *absolutely* act on it, because I know it would distract the students. I don't just stand up and start doing star jumps in the middle of a history paper! \n\nInstead, I’ll find little ways to manage it that aren't quite so obvious. I might wiggle my toes furiously inside my shoes—I swear by Clarks, by the way, they have excellent arch support, which is absolutely vital when you've been teaching as long as I have and the school insists on those terribly hard linoleum floors. Or I might just sort of tense and relax my calf muscles under the desk where no one can see, or subtly bounce my heel. \n\nSo, I can suppress it if the situation demands it. It just takes a rather exhausting amount of mental effort to do so. It feels a bit like holding a beach ball underwater, if that makes sense? You can do it, but you're constantly fighting the pressure of it wanting to pop back up, and the moment you stop paying attention, up it comes. But an uncontrollable urge that I simply *must* act on regardless of the consequences? No, no, I wouldn't say it's quite like that. I do still have some manners, after all!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. He could probably sleep through a brass band parading through our bedroom, or at the very least, a moderately sized earthquake. But I am quite certain that if I were actually kicking him or jerking my legs about violently in the middle of the night, he’d eventually notice and say something. He’s rather particular about his sleep environment, you see. If I so much as turn the page of my book too loudly—because I do often have to read to try and quiet my mind, usually a historical biography or something quite dense, just to tire my eyes out—he’ll grumble and pull the duvet over his ears. \n\nSo a physical kick? Or a sudden jerk? Oh, I would absolutely hear about that the next morning over breakfast. He’d be terribly grumpy about it while buttering his toast, I assure you, and he wouldn't let me forget it. He'd probably suggest we get separate blankets like they do in Scandinavia—I read an article about that recently, actually, how it saves marriages, though I think it looks terribly untidy when you make the bed. \n\nBut no, he has never once complained about me twitching or jerking in my sleep. And I’ve never woken myself up doing it, either. I really do think that once I'm finally horizontal and the lights are out, my body is just completely still. It's almost a relief to my legs, I suppose. Even if my brain is still rattling on, drafting lesson plans or worrying about the state of the national curriculum, my limbs are perfectly quiet. So, no, no sudden movements or jerking at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this restless feeling in your legs even when you're not required to sit still, such as when you're at home doing chores or engaging in an activity you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not at all. If I'm up and about, doing chores or what have you, I don't feel that restlessness in the slightest. It’s really quite the opposite, actually! When I’m moving, I feel entirely... well, normal, I suppose. \n\nTake the weekends, for example. Saturday mornings are my time for getting the house in order, and I am constantly on my feet. I’ll be up and down the stairs with the laundry, hoovering the sitting room, and then there’s the ironing. Arthur insists on wearing these dreadful heavy cotton shirts that seem to wrinkle if you so much as look at them crossly, so I can easily spend a good hour or more just stood at the ironing board. And during all of that, my legs are perfectly fine. There’s no buzzing, no urge to jiggle or shift about, because, well, I’m already moving, aren't I? \n\nIt’s exactly the same with things I actually enjoy doing, too. I’m quite a keen baker—I find it terribly relaxing to follow a recipe, it’s all so wonderfully precise, quite unlike marking Year 9 essays where you have to try and decipher what on earth they actually meant to say about the industrial revolution. So if I’m in the kitchen on a Sunday afternoon, measuring out flour for a Victoria sponge, bustling between the counter and the oven, I don’t feel any of that dreadful trapped feeling in my legs. Or if I'm out in the garden pruning the roses, for instance. I can spend hours out there, pottering about from the flowerbeds to the shed, and my body is perfectly content. \n\nIt really does just come back to the sitting, you see. If I’m allowed to be in motion, to be doing something physical, whether it’s wiping down the whiteboards at the end of the school day or just dusting the mantelpiece at home, the restlessness completely vanishes. It’s as if my body just wants to be put to use, and as long as I’m obliging it, it doesn’t give me a spot of bother. So no, I certainly don't get that feeling when I'm active or engaged in my chores. It's only when I'm forced to stop!\n\nDoctor: Would you say the urge to move your legs is something you feel you absolutely must act on, even if you know it might seem unnecessary or inconvenient?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say 'absolutely must', no. It’s not as if my legs have a mind of their own and will just leap up and start tap-dancing without my permission! It’s more of a... a very strong preference, I suppose you could say. \n\nI mean, if I'm in a situation where it would be completely inappropriate or highly inconvenient to move, I *can* force myself to stay still. Take exam invigilation, for example. I mentioned the mock GCSEs earlier. When you're invigilating, you have to sit at that little desk at the front of the sports hall for up to two hours in absolute, pin-drop silence. The acoustics in our school hall are dreadful, by the way, if you so much as drop a biro it sounds like a gunshot. But anyway, when I’m sitting there, I do get that familiar buzzing, that desperate feeling of wanting to stretch or pace up and down the aisles. But I don't *absolutely* act on it, because I know it would distract the students. I don't just stand up and start doing star jumps in the middle of a history paper! \n\nInstead, I’ll find little ways to manage it that aren't quite so obvious. I might wiggle my toes furiously inside my shoes—I swear by Clarks, by the way, they have excellent arch support, which is absolutely vital when you've been teaching as long as I have and the school insists on those terribly hard linoleum floors. Or I might just sort of tense and relax my calf muscles under the desk where no one can see, or subtly bounce my heel. \n\nSo, I can suppress it if the situation demands it. It just takes a rather exhausting amount of mental effort to do so. It feels a bit like holding a beach ball underwater, if that makes sense? You can do it, but you're constantly fighting the pressure of it wanting to pop back up, and the moment you stop paying attention, up it comes. But an uncontrollable urge that I simply *must* act on regardless of the consequences? No, no, I wouldn't say it's quite like that. I do still have some manners, after all!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, I can’t say that I do. Standing is quite different from sitting, isn't it? I mean, if I’m standing in a queue—say, at the Post Office on a Saturday morning, which is always an absolute nightmare because everyone is returning all those online shopping parcels... Arthur absolutely despises online shopping, by the way, he says it’s ruining the high street, and he has a point, though the high street isn't exactly what it used to be anyway, is it? But anyway, if I’m in that queue, I might feel a bit impatient, naturally. I might shift my weight from one foot to the other, mostly because even with my sensible shoes, standing in one spot on a hard tile floor can make your lower back ache just a touch at my age. But it’s not that same... that same trapped, buzzing sensation I get when I’m forced into a chair. 

Or if I’m waiting for Arthur to get ready to go out—he takes simply ages to do his tie, he insists on a perfect Windsor knot if we're going anywhere remotely nice, which is lovely but terribly time-consuming—I’ll usually just find something to do, like straightening the coats in the hallway or sorting through the post. But even if I am just standing completely still, say at the supermarket checkout while the person in front of me spends ten minutes hunting for their Nectar card at the very bottom of their handbag... I don't get that inner fidgetiness. 

I might roll my eyes internally, of course, and wish they'd hurry up, but my body feels perfectly fine. It really does seem to be strictly tied to the act of sitting down, of being folded up in a seat. Once I'm up on my feet, even if I'm not actively walking about, that particular restless energy just isn't there. So no, I haven't noticed it happening when I'm standing in line or anything of that sort.

Well, I suppose I should start by saying that I'm a secondary school teacher, so I am very used to being on my feet all day. But what actually brought me in today is... well, it's becoming quite embarrassing, to be honest. I just keep getting up when I'm supposed to be sitting down. \n\nIt happens down at the school during our staff meetings, and even in my personal life, like at dinner parties when everyone else is perfectly content just remaining seated. I’ll be sitting there, and suddenly I just feel this need to move, so I'll invent a reason to get up. I'll pop up to refill my coffee—even if my cup is still half full, which is ridiculous—or I'll suddenly decide I need to go check a handout on the other side of the room. Or sometimes I just walk to the back of the room and stand there for a while! And the thing is, none of it is ever really urgent. \n\nMy husband actually pointed it out at a dinner last week. We were at our friends' house, we had just finished the main course, and I just stood up to go look at a painting on their wall. I mean, who does that? Everyone else is sitting, having a lovely conversation, and I'm wandering around their dining room. It's just... I don't know, I'm 60 years old, people expect me to be able to sit through a normal, quiet event, but it's like my legs just decide it's time to go and I can't stay in my chair without finding some excuse to be on my feet.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what you’re feeling in your legs when you get the urge to stand up? Is it more of a restlessness, discomfort, or something else that makes it hard to stay seated?\n\nPatient: Well, it’s certainly not a pain or a physical ache, if that’s what you mean. It’s more of a... a buzzing, I suppose? Or just this incredible restlessness that sort of builds up. If I try to ignore it and just force myself to stay in the chair—because, as I said, sometimes you really just can't get up, no matter how much you want to. Like when I'm sitting at my desk grading essays. Oh, the essays. I'll be halfway through a massive stack of papers on the Industrial Revolution, trying to focus on what my students have written, and I'll suddenly realize I'm just constantly in motion. \n\nI’ll be tapping my foot against the back of the desk, just tap-tap-tapping away, or I'll be clicking my red grading pen without even realizing I'm doing it. Click, click, click. And I'm shifting my weight from one hip to the other, crossing my legs, uncrossing them, adjusting my posture... it's exhausting! \n\nIt’s the exact same thing when I’m in church on a Sunday, which is terribly embarrassing. I’ll be sitting in the pew during the vicar's sermon, and I just squirm and shift around so much that I literally have to have a stern internal monologue and remind myself to just settle down. I’ll think, 'For goodness sake, you’re a grown woman, sit still!' But it’s just this restless energy that absolutely has to go somewhere, so if I can't stand up and walk it off like I want to, it just sort of leaks out into my feet and my hands and I end up fidgeting the entire time.\n\nDoctor: Does this restless feeling in your legs tend to happen more in the evening, or after you've been sitting for a long time?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really... I mean, I wouldn't say it's an evening thing specifically, or even that it only happens after I've been sitting for ages. It's not like that at all. It's more just... whenever I'm expected to sit, really. It could be first thing in the morning! We have our morning staff briefings at 8:00 AM sharp, and sometimes I've barely sat down with my tea, and within five minutes, I'm already feeling that sort of buzzing start up in my legs. I'll be sitting there, listening to the headmaster drone on about the new attendance policy or whatever it is that day, and I'm already shifting around, thinking about getting up to look at the notice board, even though I just looked at it on my way in. \n\nAnd then, of course, it happens in the evening too, like at those dinner parties I mentioned, or if my husband and I are just trying to sit and watch a program on the television after dinner. We love those historical dramas, you know, being a history teacher and all, but even then, I can't just sink into the sofa and stay there for an hour. I'll be up checking the kettle, or deciding I need to fold the laundry right that second. \n\nSo, it's not really tied to the time of day, and I don't have to be sitting for hours and hours for it to start. It's just the very act of having to stay seated when my body clearly wants to be doing something else. It's like my internal engine is just always idling a bit too high, whether it's breakfast time or supper time, whether I've been sitting for five minutes or fifty. It's terribly frustrating, honestly, because you'd think after a long day of teaching I'd want nothing more than to just collapse into a chair and stay there, but my legs just don't seem to get the message!\n\nDoctor: Does the restless feeling in your legs get better when you stand up or move around?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does, in a way... but not like a magical cure or anything. It’s more that when I finally do get up—like when I invent that excuse to go check the coffee pot or wander over to the notice board during the staff briefing—it’s as if a pressure valve has been released, just for a moment. But it’s not as if the restlessness completely disappears forever just because I took a little walk across the room. \n\nI mean, yes, while I'm actually walking, while I'm moving, I feel much more... well, normal, I suppose. I feel like myself. When I’m pacing at the front of my classroom, lecturing about the Tudor dynasty, I feel absolutely fine! I don't even think about my legs. But the second I have to sit back down, say, if the students are taking an exam and I need to supervise from my desk... oh, it just starts right back up again. The buzzing, the tapping, the need to shift. \n\nSo, yes, the moving around definitely helps while I'm doing it. If I could just stay in motion all day, I probably wouldn't even be here talking to you! But the problem is, society expects a 60-year-old woman to sit down eventually, doesn't it? And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness in your legs, is there ever any discomfort or an uncomfortable sensation—like aching, tingling, or crawling—that makes you want to move them?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. It’s not a... a physical discomfort, if you see what I mean. Not an ache, certainly not a tingling, and definitely not that awful crawling feeling you hear people talk about. I had a colleague once, a lovely woman who taught geography, who had something like that—she said it felt like spiders in her veins, which just sounds absolutely dreadful, doesn't it? But no, it’s nothing like that for me. \n\nIt’s really just... energy. Pure, unadulterated energy that simply has no place to go. It’s not painful in the slightest, just incredibly insistent. It’s as if my legs are saying, \"Right then, we're done sitting, let's get on with it!\" even if my brain is saying, \"Please, just let me finish marking this one last essay on the French Revolution.\" There’s no physical sensation of illness or ache, just this overwhelming, bubbling drive to be doing something else, somewhere else, preferably on my feet. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this same restless energy in your legs when you're already standing or walking, or does it only happen when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... no, no, I don't believe so. In fact, I'm quite certain I don't. When I'm already up and about, I feel perfectly fine. It’s... well, it's quite the opposite, actually! When I’m on my feet, that restless energy just sort of dissipates, because it’s finally being used for what it wants to do, if that makes sense. \n\nTake my weekends, for instance. My husband and I, we love to go on these long walks—we'll go down to the local historical society or just wander through the village, perhaps pop into an antique shop or two, and I could walk for absolute hours. My legs never feel restless or twitchy when we're doing that. Or at school, when I'm on break duty in the corridors. You can imagine the absolute chaos of it, teenagers everywhere, locker doors slamming, but I'm just pacing up and down, keeping an eye on things, making sure no one is loitering by the science labs, and I feel completely in my element. No buzzing, no urge to shake my leg, because, well, I'm already moving! \n\nIt truly is just the sitting down that brings it on. It's the physical constraint of it, the expectation of being trapped in a chair. The moment my bottom hits a seat, the countdown begins. But if I'm already on my feet? Oh, I'm right as rain. I can stand at the front of the room—at the smartboard, they call it now, though I must admit I still miss the old chalkboards sometimes, there was something so satisfying about writing with proper chalk—anyway, I can stand there for a double period of A-level history, walking back and forth, and I won't even think about my legs once. So no, it’s never when I’m already walking or standing. It’s entirely a sitting-down affair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself moving your legs or doing anything else automatically when you're trying to relax or fall asleep at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, well, let me think about that for a moment. When I'm trying to relax at night... no, I wouldn't say I do anything automatically with my legs then. It's actually quite the opposite, in a way. When I finally get into bed—which, I must admit, is often quite late because I always seem to find one more thing to do, one more essay to glance over, or I'll remember I need to set out my clothes for the next day, and then I'll decide my entire wardrobe needs reorganizing right then and there—but when I do finally lie down, I'm usually just exhausted. \n\nMy husband, Arthur, he's out like a light the moment his head hits the pillow. It's truly astonishing. But me, my mind might still be racing, thinking about the lesson plan for the Year 11s or whether I was too harsh on that boy in the third row who wouldn't stop talking during the presentation on the industrial revolution. But my legs? No, they aren't moving on their own. \n\nI don't find myself kicking or twitching or anything like that when I'm trying to sleep. Once I'm horizontal, the physical need to move seems to settle down. It's really just during the day, when I'm sitting up, trying to focus on something stationary, like a staff meeting or marking papers. But at night, in bed, or if I'm, say, trying to watch a program on the television—though I rarely make it through a whole hour without getting up to put the kettle on or tidy the magazines—if I am actually just lying there trying to sleep, no, I don't notice any automatic movements. I suppose my body finally realizes the day is done, even if my brain hasn't quite caught up yet. So, no, nothing like that at night.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed that when you're first trying to fall asleep, you feel the urge to move your legs even though you're lying down?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not an urge either. It really does tie back to what I was just saying, you see. Once I'm actually in the bed, lying flat, my legs seem to finally understand that the working day is over. They’ve done their pacing, they’ve walked the corridors, they’ve stood at the smartboard—so by the time I’m actually trying to fall asleep, they are perfectly content to just... stop. \n\nThere’s no crawling, no twitching, and certainly no urge to get up and march about. It’s quite the contrast to my mind, really! I'll be lying there, perfectly still physically, but mentally I'm running a marathon. I'll be re-evaluating the entire Tudor syllabus or wondering if I remembered to lock the filing cabinet in the staff room. Just the other night, for instance, I spent what must have been forty-five minutes debating with myself whether I should switch from the history textbook we've been using for the last five years to that new one the exam board recommended. Arthur was snoring away beside me, completely oblivious, and I was wide awake, staring at the ceiling, going over chapter structures in my head. \n\nBut through all of that mental gymnastics, my body itself is quite happy to remain stationary. I don't feel that bubbling need to move my legs like I do when I'm trapped in a chair during the day. It’s as if gravity does the trick, or perhaps it’s just the sheer exhaustion of being on my feet for so many hours. But no, no urge to move them at all once I'm lying down to sleep. They just feel heavy and tired, which is exactly how they ought to feel at that hour, I suppose!\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that the restlessness in your legs tends to be worse during the evening hours compared to other times of the day, especially when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's worse in the evening, no. It really just comes down to the sitting itself, regardless of what the clock on the wall says. I mean, if I'm sitting in a staff briefing at half-past eight in the morning, listening to the headmaster go on and on about the new attendance policy—which, frankly, is nearly identical to the old attendance policy, just with considerably more paperwork for us teachers—my legs are just as restless, just as desperate to get moving, as they are at eight o'clock at night. \n\nIt is true that I do a fair bit of sitting in the evenings, of course, which might make it seem that way. It's mock exam season for the GCSE students right now, so you can imagine the sheer volume of essays I'm wading through on the causes of the First World War. And yes, when I sit down on the sofa with my red pen, that familiar buzzing feeling starts up almost immediately, and I'll find myself bouncing my heel or needing to shift my position every few minutes. \n\nBut is it actually worse than during the day? No, I don't believe so. I think it's just that the evening is when I'm expected to sit still for longer, uninterrupted stretches, isn't it? I'm trying so hard to just sit and be still like a normal person relaxing, and I simply can't. \n\nSo the actual physical feeling in my legs? No, it's exactly the same intensity as it is at ten in the morning. It's just the act of being stuck in a chair that does it, morning, noon, or night.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that your legs jerk or move suddenly while you're falling asleep or during the night?\n\nPatient: No, no one has ever mentioned anything of the sort. He could probably sleep through a brass band parading through our bedroom, or at the very least, a moderately sized earthquake. He’s rather particular about his sleep environment, you see. If I so much as turn the page of my book too loudly—because I do often have to read to try and quiet my mind, usually a historical biography or something quite dense, just to tire my eyes out—he’ll grumble and pull the duvet over his ears. \n\nSo a physical kick? Or a sudden jerk? Oh, I would absolutely hear about that the next morning over breakfast. He’d be terribly grumpy about it while buttering his toast, I assure you, and he wouldn't let me forget it. He'd probably suggest we get separate blankets like they do in Scandinavia—I read an article about that recently, actually, how it saves marriages, though I think it looks terribly untidy when you make the bed. \n\nBut no, he has never once complained about me twitching or jerking in my sleep. If I'm up and about, doing chores or what have you, I don't feel that restlessness in the slightest. It’s really quite the opposite, actually! When I’m moving, I feel entirely... well, normal, I suppose. \n\nTake the weekends, for example. Saturday mornings are my time for getting the house in order, and I am constantly on my feet. I’m quite a keen baker—I find it terribly relaxing to follow a recipe, it’s all so wonderfully precise, quite unlike marking Year 9 essays where you have to try and decipher what on earth they actually meant to say about the industrial revolution. So if I’m in the kitchen on a Sunday afternoon, measuring out flour for a Victoria sponge, bustling between the counter and the oven, I don’t feel any of that dreadful trapped feeling in my legs. Or if I'm out in the garden pruning the roses, for instance. I can spend hours out there, pottering about from the flowerbeds to the shed, and my body is perfectly content. \n\nIt really does just come back to the sitting, you see. If I’m allowed to be in motion, to be doing something physical, whether it’s wiping down the whiteboards at the end of the school day or just dusting the mantelpiece at home, the restlessness completely vanishes. It’s as if my body just wants to be put to use, and as long as I’m obliging it, it doesn’t give me a spot of bother. So no, I certainly don't get that feeling when I'm active or engaged in my chores. The acoustics in our school hall are dreadful, by the way, if you so much as drop a biro it sounds like a gunshot. I don't just stand up and start doing star jumps in the middle of a history paper! \n\nInstead, I’ll find little ways to manage it that aren't quite so obvious. You can do it, but you're constantly fighting the pressure of it wanting to pop back up, and the moment you stop paying attention, up it comes. Arthur absolutely despises online shopping, by the way, he says it’s ruining the high street, and he has a point, though the high street isn't exactly what it used to be anyway, is it? But anyway, if I’m in that queue, I might feel a bit impatient, naturally. I might shift my weight from one foot to the other, mostly because even with my sensible shoes, standing in one spot on a hard tile floor can make your lower back ache just a touch at my age. But it’s not that same... that same trapped, buzzing sensation I get when I’m forced into a chair. \n\nOr if I’m waiting for Arthur to get ready to go out—he takes simply ages to do his tie, he insists on a perfect Windsor knot if we're going anywhere remotely nice, which is lovely but terribly time-consuming—I’ll usually just find something to do, like straightening the coats in the hallway or sorting through the post. It really does seem to be strictly tied to the act of sitting down, of being folded up in a seat. Once I'm up on my feet, even if I'm not actively walking about, that particular restless energy just isn't there. And the moment I do, it’s like my body forgets I just walked around the room and insists we need to go again. It’s a very temporary fix, getting up. It helps in the moment, but it doesn't actually solve the problem of not being able to just sit and relax like a normal person.\n\nDoctor: When you feel that restlessness in your legs, is there ever any discomfort or an uncomfortable sensation—like aching, tingling, or crawling—that makes you want to move them?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that at all. It’s not a... a physical discomfort, if you see what I mean. Not an ache, certainly not a tingling, and definitely not that awful crawling feeling you hear people talk about. I had a colleague once, a lovely woman who taught geography, who had something like that—she said it felt like spiders in her veins, which just sounds absolutely dreadful, doesn't it? But no, it’s nothing like that for me. \n\nIt’s really just... energy. Pure, unadulterated energy that simply has no place to go. It’s not painful in the slightest, just incredibly insistent. It’s as if my legs are saying, \"Right then, we're done sitting, let's get on with it!\" even if my brain is saying, \"Please, just let me finish marking this one last essay on the French Revolution.\" There’s no physical sensation of illness or ache, just this overwhelming, bubbling drive to be doing something else, somewhere else, preferably on my feet. I suppose the only discomfort is the mental frustration of it all, really, having to constantly fight my own body's desire to just get up and go. But physically? No, no crawling or tingling whatsoever. Just the buzzing. Always the buzzing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this same restless energy in your legs when you're already standing or walking, or does it only happen when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... no, no, I don't believe so. In fact, I'm quite certain I don't. When I'm already up and about, I feel perfectly fine. It’s... well, it's quite the opposite, actually! When I’m on my feet, that restless energy just sort of dissipates, because it’s finally being used for what it wants to do, if that makes sense. \n\nTake my weekends, for instance. My husband and I, we love to go on these long walks—we'll go down to the local historical society or just wander through the village, perhaps pop into an antique shop or two, and I could walk for absolute hours. My legs never feel restless or twitchy when we're doing that. Or at school, when I'm on break duty in the corridors. You can imagine the absolute chaos of it, teenagers everywhere, locker doors slamming, but I'm just pacing up and down, keeping an eye on things, making sure no one is loitering by the science labs, and I feel completely in my element. No buzzing, no urge to shake my leg, because, well, I'm already moving! \n\nIt truly is just the sitting down that brings it on. It's the physical constraint of it, the expectation of being trapped in a chair. The moment my bottom hits a seat, the countdown begins. But if I'm already on my feet? Oh, I'm right as rain. I can stand at the front of the room—at the smartboard, they call it now, though I must admit I still miss the old chalkboards sometimes, there was something so satisfying about writing with proper chalk—anyway, I can stand there for a double period of A-level history, walking back and forth, and I won't even think about my legs once. So no, it’s never when I’m already walking or standing. It’s entirely a sitting-down affair.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself moving your legs or doing anything else automatically when you're trying to relax or fall asleep at night?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, well, let me think about that for a moment. When I'm trying to relax at night... no, I wouldn't say I do anything automatically with my legs then. It's actually quite the opposite, in a way. When I finally get into bed—which, I must admit, is often quite late because I always seem to find one more thing to do, one more essay to glance over, or I'll remember I need to set out my clothes for the next day, and then I'll decide my entire wardrobe needs reorganizing right then and there—but when I do finally lie down, I'm usually just exhausted. \n\nMy husband, Arthur, he's out like a light the moment his head hits the pillow. It's truly astonishing. But me, my mind might still be racing, thinking about the lesson plan for the Year 11s or whether I was too harsh on that boy in the third row who wouldn't stop talking during the presentation on the industrial revolution. But my legs? No, they aren't moving on their own. \n\nI don't find myself kicking or twitching or anything like that when I'm trying to sleep. Once I'm horizontal, the physical need to move seems to settle down. It's really just during the day, when I'm sitting up, trying to focus on something stationary, like a staff meeting or marking papers. But at night, in bed, or if I'm, say, trying to watch a program on the television—though I rarely make it through a whole hour without getting up to put the kettle on or tidy the magazines—if I am actually just lying there trying to sleep, no, I don't notice any automatic movements. I suppose my body finally realizes the day is done, even if my brain hasn't quite caught up yet. So, no, nothing like that at night.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever noticed that when you're first trying to fall asleep, you feel the urge to move your legs even though you're lying down?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not an urge either. It really does tie back to what I was just saying, you see. Once I'm actually in the bed, lying flat, my legs seem to finally understand that the working day is over. They’ve done their pacing, they’ve walked the corridors, they’ve stood at the smartboard—so by the time I’m actually trying to fall asleep, they are perfectly content to just... stop. \n\nThere’s no crawling, no twitching, and certainly no urge to get up and march about. It’s quite the contrast to my mind, really! I'll be lying there, perfectly still physically, but mentally I'm running a marathon. I'll be re-evaluating the entire Tudor syllabus or wondering if I remembered to lock the filing cabinet in the staff room. Just the other night, for instance, I spent what must have been forty-five minutes debating with myself whether I should switch from the history textbook we've been using for the last five years to that new one the exam board recommended. Arthur was snoring away beside me, completely oblivious, and I was wide awake, staring at the ceiling, going over chapter structures in my head. \n\nBut through all of that mental gymnastics, my body itself is quite happy to remain stationary. I don't feel that bubbling need to move my legs like I do when I'm trapped in a chair during the day. It’s as if gravity does the trick, or perhaps it’s just the sheer exhaustion of being on my feet for so many hours. But no, no urge to move them at all once I'm lying down to sleep. They just feel heavy and tired, which is exactly how they ought to feel at that hour, I suppose!\n\nDoctor: Do you notice that the restlessness in your legs tends to be worse during the evening hours compared to other times of the day, especially when you're sitting down?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say it's worse in the evening, no. It really just comes down to the sitting itself, regardless of what the clock on the wall says. I mean, if I'm sitting in a staff briefing at half-past eight in the morning, listening to the headmaster go on and on about the new attendance policy—which, frankly, is nearly identical to the old attendance policy, just with considerably more paperwork for us teachers—my legs are just as restless, just as desperate to get moving, as they are at eight o'clock at night. \n\nIt is true that I do a fair bit of sitting in the evenings, of course, which might make it seem that way. After dinner, Arthur and I usually move into the sitting room. He'll have his newspaper or perhaps a crossword, and I'll invariably have a massive stack of marking on my lap. It's mock exam season for the GCSE students right now, so you can imagine the sheer volume of essays I'm wading through on the causes of the First World War. And yes, when I sit down on the sofa with my red pen, that familiar buzzing feeling starts up almost immediately, and I'll find myself bouncing my heel or needing to shift my position every few minutes. \n\nBut is it actually worse than during the day? No, I don't believe so. I think it's just that the evening is when I'm expected to sit still for longer, uninterrupted stretches, isn't it? During the school day, if I get that restless feeling while I'm sitting at my desk, I can just pop up and walk around the classroom, check on the students' progress, or nip down to the staff room to make a quick cup of tea. But in the evening, when we're trying to watch a bit of television—we've been watching a new historical drama lately, though I must say their attention to period-accurate costumes is shockingly poor, they had a woman in a 1920s scene wearing a zipper that clearly wasn't invented until much later—but anyway, when I'm supposed to be just relaxing on the sofa, the restlessness is perhaps more noticeable to me. I'm trying so hard to just sit and be still like a normal person relaxing, and I simply can't. \n\nSo the actual physical feeling in my legs? No, it's exactly the same intensity as it is at ten in the morning. It's just the act of being stuck in a chair that does it, morning, noon, or night.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told that your legs jerk or move suddenly while you're falling asleep or during the night?\n\nPatient: No, no one has ever mentioned anything of the sort. And by no one, I really mean Arthur, of course, since he’s the only one who would be in a position to know! \n\nNow, as I mentioned earlier, Arthur is an incredibly heavy sleeper. He really is. He could probably sleep through a brass band parading through our bedroom, or at the very least, a moderately sized earthquake. But I am quite certain that if I were actually kicking him or jerking my legs about violently in the middle of the night, he’d eventually notice and say something. He’s rather particular about his sleep environment, you see. If I so much as turn the page of my book too loudly—because I do often have to read to try and quiet my mind, usually a historical biography or something quite dense, just to tire my eyes out—he’ll grumble and pull the duvet over his ears. \n\nSo a physical kick? Or a sudden jerk? Oh, I would absolutely hear about that the next morning over breakfast. He’d be terribly grumpy about it while buttering his toast, I assure you, and he wouldn't let me forget it. He'd probably suggest we get separate blankets like they do in Scandinavia—I read an article about that recently, actually, how it saves marriages, though I think it looks terribly untidy when you make the bed. \n\nBut no, he has never once complained about me twitching or jerking in my sleep. And I’ve never woken myself up doing it, either. I really do think that once I'm finally horizontal and the lights are out, my body is just completely still. It's almost a relief to my legs, I suppose. Even if my brain is still rattling on, drafting lesson plans or worrying about the state of the national curriculum, my limbs are perfectly quiet. So, no, no sudden movements or jerking at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel this restless feeling in your legs even when you're not required to sit still, such as when you're at home doing chores or engaging in an activity you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not at all. If I'm up and about, doing chores or what have you, I don't feel that restlessness in the slightest. It’s really quite the opposite, actually! When I’m moving, I feel entirely... well, normal, I suppose. \n\nTake the weekends, for example. Saturday mornings are my time for getting the house in order, and I am constantly on my feet. I’ll be up and down the stairs with the laundry, hoovering the sitting room, and then there’s the ironing. Arthur insists on wearing these dreadful heavy cotton shirts that seem to wrinkle if you so much as look at them crossly, so I can easily spend a good hour or more just stood at the ironing board. And during all of that, my legs are perfectly fine. There’s no buzzing, no urge to jiggle or shift about, because, well, I’m already moving, aren't I? \n\nIt’s exactly the same with things I actually enjoy doing, too. I’m quite a keen baker—I find it terribly relaxing to follow a recipe, it’s all so wonderfully precise, quite unlike marking Year 9 essays where you have to try and decipher what on earth they actually meant to say about the industrial revolution. So if I’m in the kitchen on a Sunday afternoon, measuring out flour for a Victoria sponge, bustling between the counter and the oven, I don’t feel any of that dreadful trapped feeling in my legs. Or if I'm out in the garden pruning the roses, for instance. I can spend hours out there, pottering about from the flowerbeds to the shed, and my body is perfectly content. \n\nIt really does just come back to the sitting, you see. If I’m allowed to be in motion, to be doing something physical, whether it’s wiping down the whiteboards at the end of the school day or just dusting the mantelpiece at home, the restlessness completely vanishes. It’s as if my body just wants to be put to use, and as long as I’m obliging it, it doesn’t give me a spot of bother. So no, I certainly don't get that feeling when I'm active or engaged in my chores. It's only when I'm forced to stop!\n\nDoctor: Would you say the urge to move your legs is something you feel you absolutely must act on, even if you know it might seem unnecessary or inconvenient?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I wouldn't say 'absolutely must', no. It’s not as if my legs have a mind of their own and will just leap up and start tap-dancing without my permission! It’s more of a... a very strong preference, I suppose you could say. \n\nI mean, if I'm in a situation where it would be completely inappropriate or highly inconvenient to move, I *can* force myself to stay still. Take exam invigilation, for example. I mentioned the mock GCSEs earlier. When you're invigilating, you have to sit at that little desk at the front of the sports hall for up to two hours in absolute, pin-drop silence. The acoustics in our school hall are dreadful, by the way, if you so much as drop a biro it sounds like a gunshot. But anyway, when I’m sitting there, I do get that familiar buzzing, that desperate feeling of wanting to stretch or pace up and down the aisles. But I don't *absolutely* act on it, because I know it would distract the students. I don't just stand up and start doing star jumps in the middle of a history paper! \n\nInstead, I’ll find little ways to manage it that aren't quite so obvious. I might wiggle my toes furiously inside my shoes—I swear by Clarks, by the way, they have excellent arch support, which is absolutely vital when you've been teaching as long as I have and the school insists on those terribly hard linoleum floors. Or I might just sort of tense and relax my calf muscles under the desk where no one can see, or subtly bounce my heel. \n\nSo, I can suppress it if the situation demands it. It just takes a rather exhausting amount of mental effort to do so. It feels a bit like holding a beach ball underwater, if that makes sense? You can do it, but you're constantly fighting the pressure of it wanting to pop back up, and the moment you stop paying attention, up it comes. But an uncontrollable urge that I simply *must* act on regardless of the consequences? No, no, I wouldn't say it's quite like that. I do still have some manners, after all!\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that same restless energy or inner fidgetiness even when you're not sitting down, for example, when you're standing in line or waiting for someone?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I can’t say that I do. Standing is quite different from sitting, isn't it? I mean, if I’m standing in a queue—say, at the Post Office on a Saturday morning, which is always an absolute nightmare because everyone is returning all those online shopping parcels... Arthur absolutely despises online shopping, by the way, he says it’s ruining the high street, and he has a point, though the high street isn't exactly what it used to be anyway, is it? But anyway, if I’m in that queue, I might feel a bit impatient, naturally. I might shift my weight from one foot to the other, mostly because even with my sensible shoes, standing in one spot on a hard tile floor can make your lower back ache just a touch at my age. But it’s not that same... that same trapped, buzzing sensation I get when I’m forced into a chair. \n\nOr if I’m waiting for Arthur to get ready to go out—he takes simply ages to do his tie, he insists on a perfect Windsor knot if we're going anywhere remotely nice, which is lovely but terribly time-consuming—I’ll usually just find something to do, like straightening the coats in the hallway or sorting through the post. But even if I am just standing completely still, say at the supermarket checkout while the person in front of me spends ten minutes hunting for their Nectar card at the very bottom of their handbag... I don't get that inner fidgetiness. \n\nI might roll my eyes internally, of course, and wish they'd hurry up, but my body feels perfectly fine. It really does seem to be strictly tied to the act of sitting down, of being folded up in a seat. Once I'm up on my feet, even if I'm not actively walking about, that particular restless energy just isn't there. So no, I haven't noticed it happening when I'm standing in line or anything of that sort.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
